 ## Learning objectives
 - Define Linux and distinguish the kernel from a complete operating system.
 - Explain the roles of user space, the kernel, and system utilities.
-- Describe GNU/Linux and the open-source model.
+- Describe GNU/Linux and the open-source model, including what open source does and does not guarantee.
 
 ## Introduction
-Linux is a family of operating systems built around the Linux kernel. It powers servers, cloud infrastructure, phones (through Android's kernel lineage), embedded devices, developer workstations, and security tools. Its command line is especially useful because it makes system behavior inspectable and automatable.
+Linux is a family of operating systems built around the Linux kernel. It runs most of the world's servers, the majority of cloud infrastructure, every Android phone (through its kernel lineage), network equipment, embedded devices, developer workstations, and nearly every serious security tool. If you plan to work in system administration, DevOps, or cybersecurity, Linux is not one option among many; it is the default environment you will operate in.
+
+Its command line is especially worth learning because it makes system behavior **inspectable** (you can ask the system exactly what it is doing) and **automatable** (anything you can type, you can script and repeat a thousand times).
 
 ## Core concepts
 
 ### Kernel and operating system
-The **kernel** is the privileged core that manages CPU scheduling, memory, devices, filesystems, networking, and system calls. Applications normally run in **user space** and request kernel services through system calls.
+The **kernel** is the privileged core of the system. It manages CPU scheduling, memory, devices and their drivers, filesystems, networking, and the system calls through which programs request services. When people say "Linux" precisely, they often mean this kernel alone.
+
+Applications normally run in **user space**, a protected mode where they cannot touch hardware or each other's memory directly. When a program wants to read a file, open a network connection, or start another program, it asks the kernel through a **system call**, and the kernel decides whether to allow it.
 
-A usable Linux system also needs user-space components: a C library, command-line utilities, service manager, package manager, libraries, applications, and often a graphical environment. A **distribution** integrates and maintains these components.
+A usable operating system needs much more than a kernel: a C library, command-line utilities, an init and service manager, a package manager, libraries, applications, and often a graphical environment. A **distribution** integrates all of these around the kernel and maintains them as a coherent whole. Ubuntu, Debian, Fedora, and Arch are distributions; "Linux" underneath each of them is the same kernel project.
 
 ### GNU/Linux
-GNU is a long-running free-software project that provides many foundational user-space tools, including shells and core utilities. Many general-purpose systems combine the Linux kernel with GNU tools, so **GNU/Linux** is a technically descriptive name. Not every Linux-based system uses the same GNU components.
+GNU is a long-running free-software project that provides many foundational user-space tools, including the Bash shell and the core utilities (`ls`, `cp`, `grep`, and friends). Many general-purpose systems combine the Linux kernel with GNU tools, so **GNU/Linux** is a technically descriptive name for those systems. Not every Linux-based system uses GNU components: Android and Alpine Linux, for example, do not.
 
 ### High-level architecture
-1. Hardware provides CPU, memory, storage, and network devices.
-2. The kernel manages resources and exposes system calls.
-3. System services provide background functionality.
-4. Libraries and command-line tools offer reusable interfaces.
-5. User applications provide interactive or automated workflows.
+From the metal up:
+
+1. **Hardware** provides CPU, memory, storage, and network devices.
+2. The **kernel** manages those resources and exposes system calls.
+3. **System services** (daemons) provide background functionality like logging, networking, and scheduling.
+4. **Libraries and command-line tools** offer reusable interfaces for programs and people.
+5. **User applications** provide interactive or automated workflows.
+
+Keep this stack in mind. When something breaks, your first diagnostic question is always "which layer owns this behavior?"
 
 ### Open source
-Open-source software makes source code available under licenses that grant defined rights to use, inspect, modify, and redistribute it. Open source does not mean that every program is secure, free of charge, or free of bugs. Security still depends on design, maintenance, configuration, and timely updates.
+Open-source software publishes its source code under licenses that grant defined rights to use, inspect, modify, and redistribute it. Practically, this means you can read exactly what your system does, fix it, and share the fix.
+
+Open source does **not** mean that every program is secure, free of charge, or free of bugs. Security still depends on design, maintenance, configuration, and timely updates. A famous component can have a famous vulnerability; the open-source advantage is that the problem can be found, disclosed, and patched in public, and that you can verify the fix yourself.
 
 ## Commands and practical examples
+Two commands identify almost any Linux system:
+
 ```bash
-uname -a       # kernel and machine information
-uname -r       # kernel release
+uname -a       # kernel name, release, machine architecture, and more
+uname -r       # kernel release only
 cat /etc/os-release
 ```
-`uname` reports kernel and hardware-related information. `/etc/os-release` is a common, distribution-provided identification file. Its exact values differ by system.
+
+Sample output (yours will differ):
 
 ```text
+$ uname -r
+6.8.0-45-generic
+
+$ cat /etc/os-release
 NAME="Ubuntu"
-VERSION="24.04 LTS ..."
+VERSION="24.04 LTS (Noble Numbat)"
 ID=ubuntu
+VERSION_ID="24.04"
+```
+
+Notice the two different answers: `uname` describes the **kernel**, while `/etc/os-release` describes the **distribution**. A system running kernel 6.8 can be Ubuntu, Debian, Fedora, or a container image; the kernel release alone does not tell you. Treat all version numbers in this course as representative examples, not values your system must match.
+

+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Identify your environment: print your kernel release, your machine architecture, and your distribution's name and version, using the commands from this lesson.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+uname -r
+uname -m
+grep -E '^(NAME|VERSION)=' /etc/os-release
+```
+
+Typical output:
+
+```text
+6.8.0-45-generic
+x86_64
+NAME="Ubuntu"
+VERSION="24.04 LTS (Noble Numbat)"
+```
+
+`uname -m` prints the machine architecture (`x86_64`, `aarch64`, ...). Filtering `/etc/os-release` with `grep` keeps the output to the two fields you need; you will learn `grep` properly in module 08.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Open the manual page for `uname` and find the option that prints *only* the operating system name. Run it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Run `man uname`, search for "operating system" with `/`, and you will find `-o, --operating-system`:
+
+```bash
+uname -o
 ```
-Treat this as representative output, not a required version.

-   - Expected result: You can state the kernel release and distribution name.

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+(a) The kernel enforces file permissions when the program's system call requests the open. (b) The kernel, specifically a missing or broken device driver or firmware. (c) A user-space utility (`ls` from GNU coreutils) making system calls the kernel answers.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** A friend says "I installed Linux 24.04." What is imprecise about that sentence?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+24.04 is a *distribution* release (Ubuntu 24.04), not a Linux kernel version. The kernel on that system has its own version, shown by `uname -r`.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Why can a normal program not read directly from the disk hardware, and what does it do instead?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Programs run in user space without direct hardware access. They request the operation through a system call, and the kernel performs it (or refuses it) on their behalf.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Does "open source" mean "secure"? Explain in one sentence.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. Open source means the code can be inspected and modified under its license; security still depends on design, maintenance, configuration, and updates.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Calling every Linux distribution simply “the Linux kernel.” A distribution includes much more.
-- Assuming open source guarantees security. Review maintenance activity and configuration.
-- Confusing the kernel version with the distribution release.
+- Calling every distribution simply "the Linux kernel." A distribution is the kernel plus an entire user space and support model.
+- Assuming open source guarantees security. Review maintenance activity and configuration instead.
+- Confusing the kernel version (`uname -r`) with the distribution release (`/etc/os-release`).
+- Believing Linux is only for servers: it is also phones, routers, cars, and the machine you may be reading this on.
 
 ## Key takeaways
 - Linux is the kernel; a distribution packages it with user-space software.
-- The kernel manages hardware resources and provides system interfaces.
+- The kernel manages hardware resources and provides system calls; everything else asks it for services.
 - Open source enables inspection and collaboration, not automatic correctness.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Recognize kernel, user-space, libraries, services, and applications.
+- Recognize the kernel, user space, libraries, services, and applications as separate layers.
 - Understand privileged and unprivileged execution at a high level.
-- Relate open-source development to security maintenance.
+- Relate open-source development practices to real security maintenance.
 
 ## Introduction
-Understanding the layers of a Linux system helps troubleshoot problems. If a network interface is missing, for example, the cause may be a kernel driver, firmware, network service, configuration file, or application—not necessarily the terminal command you typed.
+Understanding the layers of a Linux system turns troubleshooting from guessing into reasoning. If a network interface is missing, the cause may be a kernel driver, missing firmware, a stopped network service, a configuration file, or the application itself, and each possibility belongs to a different layer with different tools. This lesson gives you the map; later modules give you the tools for each layer.
 
 ## Core concepts
 - **Hardware:** physical or virtual CPU, memory, storage, and peripherals.
-- **Kernel space:** privileged code with direct resource-management responsibilities.
-- **User space:** ordinary programs, including shells and services.
-- **System call:** a controlled entry point through which a process requests a kernel operation.
-- **Daemon/service:** a background program that provides a function such as logging or networking.
-- **Library:** reusable code used by programs.
+- **Kernel space:** privileged code with direct resource-management responsibilities. A bug here can crash the whole machine.
+- **User space:** ordinary programs, including your shell and system services. A bug here crashes one process; the kernel cleans up.
+- **System call:** a controlled entry point through which a process requests a kernel operation: open a file, map memory, send a packet, create a process.
+- **Daemon/service:** a background program that provides a function such as logging (`rsyslog`, `systemd-journald`), name resolution, or remote access (`sshd`).
+- **Library:** reusable code linked into programs. The C library (`glibc` on most systems) sits under nearly everything, wrapping raw system calls in functions programs actually use.
 
-A process normally has its own virtual address space. The kernel enforces boundaries between processes and controls access to resources. Root privileges grant broad administrative authority but do not make every operation safe.
+A process normally has its own virtual address space. The kernel enforces boundaries between processes and controls access to resources, so one user's program cannot read another's memory or files without permission. The **root** account is the exception: it bypasses most of these checks, which is exactly why daily work should happen in a normal account. Root privileges grant broad administrative authority but do not make any operation safe; one mistaken command as root can destroy the whole system, while the same command as a normal user can only destroy what that user owns.
 
-Open-source projects commonly publish source, issue trackers, release notes, and security advisories. A responsible administrator tracks supported releases and applies updates through trusted repositories.
+### Open source as a maintenance model
+Open-source projects commonly publish their source, issue trackers, release notes, and security advisories. A responsible administrator tracks supported releases and applies updates through the distribution's trusted repositories, which are signed and verified by the package manager (module 09). The practical loop looks like this:
+
+1. A vulnerability is reported and assigned an identifier (a CVE).
+2. The project fixes it and the distribution backports the fix to its supported releases.
+3. Your package manager offers the patched version as an ordinary update.
+4. You apply it, and your system is protected without changing how you work.
+
+This is why "is this project actively maintained?" is a security question, not just a preference.
 
 ## Commands and practical examples
+These commands inspect different layers. Some may be absent in minimal environments; install them with your distribution's package manager.
+
+```bash
+uname -r        # kernel release
+lscpu           # CPU architecture and topology
+free -h         # memory, human-readable
+lsblk           # block devices (disks and partitions)
+```
+
+Sample output:
+
+```text
+$ free -h
+               total        used        free      shared  buff/cache   available
+Mem:           7.7Gi       1.2Gi       5.4Gi        45Mi       1.1Gi       6.3Gi
+Swap:          2.0Gi          0B       2.0Gi
+```
+
+```bash
+systemctl --version
+```
+
+This reports the systemd version when systemd is installed as the init system and service manager. It is common (Ubuntu, Debian, Fedora, RHEL, Arch) but not universal: WSL sessions and some container or minimal environments use different arrangements, and you will see `systemctl` fail there even though the system is perfectly healthy. In module 07 you will use `systemctl` to manage services.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Run `lscpu`, `free -h`, and `lsblk`. For each one, write down whether it describes hardware, the kernel, or user-space software.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+`lscpu` and `lsblk` describe **hardware** (CPU and disks) as the kernel sees them. `free -h` reports **kernel** memory accounting. All three commands are themselves **user-space** programs reading kernel interfaces, mostly virtual files under `/proc` and `/sys`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Peek at the kernel's own view of your machine: print the contents of `/proc/version` and the first five lines of `/proc/cpuinfo`.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
 ```bash
-uname -r
-lscpu
-free -h
-lsblk
+cat /proc/version
+head -n 5 /proc/cpuinfo
+```
+
+Typical output:
+
+```text
+Linux version 6.8.0-45-generic (buildd@...) ...
+processor	: 0
+vendor_id	: GenuineIntel
+...
 ```
-These commands inspect the kernel release, CPU, memory, and block devices. Some may be absent in minimal environments; install their package using your distribution's package manager.
+
+`/proc` is a virtual filesystem the kernel maintains: the "files" do not exist on disk; the kernel generates their contents on read. It is one of the cleanest demonstrations of the kernel/user-space boundary.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Check whether your system uses systemd, and if it does, list how many services are currently running.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
 
 ```bash
 systemctl --version
+systemctl list-units --type=service --state=running | head
 ```
-This reports the systemd version when systemd is installed. WSL containers and non-systemd distributions may use a different init/service arrangement.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Run `lscpu`, `free -h`, and `lsblk` where available.
-- Identify which output describes hardware and which describes kernel or service software.
-- In a VM, compare output before and after changing its allocated RAM (shut the VM down before changing hardware settings).
+If the first command prints a version, systemd is present; the second lists running services with a count at the bottom. If `systemctl` is missing or errors (common in WSL and containers), your environment uses a different init arrangement and module 07 will note the differences.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Your browser crashes. Why does the whole system usually survive?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The browser runs as an unprivileged user-space process. The kernel isolates it, so when it dies the kernel reclaims its memory and keeps everything else running.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Why is doing daily work as root dangerous even if you "know what you are doing"?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Root bypasses the permission checks that limit mistakes. One wrong command (a typo in an `rm` path, a redirected write over a system file) can damage the entire system; as a normal user the same typo can only touch files you own.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Your distribution offers an update described as a security fix for a library. Nothing on your desktop looks broken. Should you apply it, and why?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Yes. Security fixes patch vulnerabilities that are not visible in normal use. Applying signed repository updates promptly is the core maintenance habit of a secure system.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Assuming all Linux systems use systemd.
-- Assuming a command exists on every minimal installation.
+- Assuming a command exists on every minimal installation; check and install instead.
 - Treating root as a normal account for daily browsing and development.
+- Ignoring updates because "everything works", which is precisely when known vulnerabilities stay open.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Linux is layered. Troubleshooting is easier when you identify which layer owns the behavior.
+Linux is layered: hardware, kernel, services, libraries, applications. Troubleshooting is easier, and safer, when you first identify which layer owns the behavior. Root is a tool for administration, not a lifestyle.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain what a distribution provides.
+- Explain what a distribution provides beyond the kernel.
 - Compare Ubuntu, Debian, Kali, Fedora, Arch, and other families.
-- Choose an appropriate environment for a learning or operational task.
+- Choose an appropriate environment for a learning or operational task, and defend the choice.
 
 ## Introduction
-A Linux distribution combines a kernel, user-space tools, package repositories, installer, defaults, and support model. Different distributions optimize for stability, rapid updates, enterprise support, simplicity, or specialist workflows.
+A Linux distribution combines the kernel, user-space tools, package repositories, an installer, default configuration, documentation, and a support model. Different distributions optimize for different things: stability, rapid updates, enterprise support, simplicity, or specialist workflows. There is no "best" distribution, only good and bad matches between a distribution's model and your purpose.
+
+Choosing well matters more than beginners expect, because your distribution decides your package manager, your default configurations, how quickly you receive updates, and how long the system is supported.
+
+## What a distribution actually decides for you
+- **Package format and manager:** `.deb` with APT (Debian family) versus `.rpm` with DNF (Fedora/RHEL family) versus pacman (Arch). Commands and package names differ between families.
+- **Release model:** fixed releases with years of support (Debian, Ubuntu LTS) versus rolling releases that update continuously (Arch).
+- **Defaults:** which desktop, init system, firewall, and security policies ship enabled.
+- **Support lifecycle:** how long security updates are provided before you must upgrade.
 
 ## Distribution families
 
@@ -22,37 +30,104 @@ A Linux distribution combines a kernel, user-space tools, package repositories,
 | Linux Mint | Ubuntu/Debian-based | Beginner-friendly desktop |
 | openSUSE | RPM, zypper | Workstations and enterprise-oriented systems |
 
+Family relationships matter: Ubuntu is built from Debian, Mint from Ubuntu, so an `apt` command usually works across all three. It will do nothing useful on Fedora or Arch.
+
 ### How to choose
-- For a first Linux system, Ubuntu LTS or Linux Mint is approachable.
-- For stable servers, Debian or an enterprise distribution may be appropriate.
-- Kali is a specialist toolkit, not a shortcut to learning Linux. Use it in a lab and understand the underlying tools.
-- Arch offers control and documentation but expects more hands-on maintenance.
-- Fedora favors relatively recent software and technologies.
+- For a **first Linux system**, Ubuntu LTS or Linux Mint is approachable, well documented, and widely answered about online.
+- For **stable servers**, Debian or an enterprise distribution (RHEL or a rebuild like Rocky/Alma) prioritizes predictability over newness.
+- For **security labs**, Kali packages hundreds of testing tools. It is a specialist toolkit, not a shortcut to learning Linux, and not a sensible daily desktop; use it in a lab VM and understand the underlying tools.
+- For **learning internals**, Arch offers total control and the best wiki in Linux, but expects hands-on maintenance.
+- For a **modern developer workstation**, Fedora ships relatively recent software with a predictable cadence.
 
-A distribution's suitability depends on support lifecycle, hardware, organizational policy, and workload—not popularity alone.
+A distribution's suitability depends on its support lifecycle, your hardware, organizational policy, and workload, not on popularity or aesthetics.
 
 ## Commands
 ```bash
 cat /etc/os-release
 cat /etc/debian_version 2>/dev/null
 ```
-The first is the portable starting point on many distributions. The second is Debian-family-specific and may not exist elsewhere.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-1. Identify your current distribution and version.
-2. Find its official documentation and support lifecycle.
-3. Explain why Kali and Ubuntu are not interchangeable defaults for every server.
+Sample output:
+
+```text
+$ cat /etc/os-release
+NAME="Ubuntu"
+VERSION="24.04 LTS (Noble Numbat)"
+ID=ubuntu
+ID_LIKE=debian
+```
+
+The first command is the portable starting point on almost every distribution; note `ID_LIKE=debian`, which records the family relationship. The second is Debian-family-specific and will not exist on Fedora or Arch. The `2>/dev/null` silences the error when the file is absent; module 08 explains redirection properly.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal (and browser for the research tasks). Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Identify your current distribution, its version, and its family from `/etc/os-release` alone.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+cat /etc/os-release
+```
+
+Read `NAME` and `VERSION` for the distribution, and `ID` / `ID_LIKE` for the family. `ID=fedora` with no `ID_LIKE` means Fedora itself; `ID=ubuntu` with `ID_LIKE=debian` means the Debian family.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Find your distribution's official support lifecycle page and write down the date your current release stops receiving security updates. (Search for "<distribution> release cycle" or "EOL".)
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+For Ubuntu, the lifecycle is published on ubuntu.com/about/release-cycle: LTS releases receive five years of standard security maintenance. For Debian, see debian.org/releases. The habit that matters: before deploying anything, confirm the release is still inside its support window.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** In two or three sentences, explain to a junior colleague why Kali and Ubuntu are not interchangeable defaults for every server.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Kali is a Debian-based toolkit for authorized security testing: it ships hundreds of attack tools, defaults tuned for labs, and a threat model that assumes a temporary, controlled environment. Ubuntu is a general-purpose system with long support, server tooling, and conservative defaults. Running Kali as a server exposes unnecessary tools and weak defaults; running Ubuntu as a testing platform means assembling the toolkit yourself.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** You find a tutorial that says `sudo dnf install nginx`. You are on Ubuntu. What do you do?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Translate it to your family: `sudo apt install nginx`. `dnf` is the Fedora/RHEL package manager; Ubuntu uses APT. Package names are usually similar but not guaranteed identical.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What two facts about a distribution matter most when choosing one for a production server?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The support lifecycle (how long security updates continue) and the release model (fixed, predictable releases versus rolling change). Package manager familiarity and organizational policy come next.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Why can an Ubuntu answer on a forum often help a Debian user, but not an Arch user?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Ubuntu is built from Debian: same package format (`.deb`), same manager (APT), similar layout. Arch uses pacman, different package names, and a rolling model, so Debian-family instructions do not transfer.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Installing Kali as a daily system without understanding its defaults and threat model.
-- Mixing package repositories from different distributions.

+- Mixing package repositories from different distributions or releases; this breaks systems in ways that are painful to undo.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Distinguish a terminal emulator, shell, and desktop environment.
-- Compare Bash with common alternative shells.
-- Select a safe Linux practice environment.
+- Distinguish a terminal emulator, a shell, and a desktop environment.
+- Compare Bash with common alternative shells, and know when the differences bite.
+- Select a safe Linux practice environment for each kind of work.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A **desktop environment** provides graphical panels, windows, settings, and applications. GNOME, KDE Plasma, and Xfce are examples. A **terminal emulator** displays a text session in a graphical window. A **shell** interprets commands inside that session.
+Three things people casually call "the terminal":
 
-Bash (Bourne Again Shell) is common on Linux. Other shells include `sh`, `zsh`, `fish`, and `dash`. Shell syntax differs: Bash arrays and `[[ ... ]]` are not POSIX `sh` features. Scripts should declare the interpreter they require.
+- A **desktop environment** provides the graphical layer: panels, windows, settings, and applications. GNOME, KDE Plasma, and Xfce are the common ones. Two machines running the same distribution can look completely different because they run different desktop environments.
+- A **terminal emulator** is one application inside that desktop: it draws a text session in a window and passes your keystrokes along. GNOME Terminal, Konsole, and Alacritty are emulators.
+- A **shell** is the program inside the session that interprets your commands. This is where Bash lives.
 
-A **virtual machine (VM)** runs a guest operating system using virtualized hardware. It is useful for snapshots and isolated labs. **WSL 2** runs a Linux environment integrated with Windows and uses a real Linux kernel in a lightweight VM architecture, but service management, devices, networking, and boot behavior can differ from a conventional Linux installation.
+The desktop environment is interchangeable decoration. The shell is where your skills transfer (or fail to).
+
+### Bash and the other shells
+Bash (Bourne Again Shell) is the default interactive shell on most Linux distributions. Others you will meet:
+
+- `sh`: the POSIX standard name. On Debian/Ubuntu it is `dash`, a small, fast, strictly limited shell.
+- `zsh`: highly customizable, the default on macOS for years.
+- `fish`: friendly interactive defaults, but deliberately non-POSIX syntax.
+
+Syntax differs between them. Bash arrays, `[[ ... ]]`, and `==` inside tests are **not** POSIX `sh` features. This has a practical consequence: a script that works when you paste it into your Bash prompt can fail when run as `sh script.sh`, because `sh` may be a different, stricter program. Scripts must declare their interpreter with a shebang (`#!/usr/bin/env bash`, covered in module 12) so the right shell runs them.
+
+### VMs and WSL
+A **virtual machine (VM)** runs a guest operating system on virtualized hardware. Its killer feature for learning is **snapshots**: save the machine's state, experiment fearlessly, and roll back in seconds. A VM gives you a full, conventional Linux system: real boot process, systemd, networking you control, and isolation from your main machine.
+
+**WSL 2** runs a real Linux kernel in a lightweight VM integrated with Windows. It is excellent for command-line development and most of this course, but it differs from a conventional installation: service management, devices, networking, and boot behavior are not the same, and some labs (systemd services, firewalling, low-level networking) behave differently or not at all.

+sh -c 'x=3; [[ $x == 3 ]] && echo sh-says-yes'
+```

+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>

+

+</details>

+

+
+They changed the terminal emulator, which only displays the session. Aliases belong to the shell's configuration (for Bash, files like `~/.bashrc`). If the new emulator starts the same shell with the same startup files, the aliases survive; if it starts a different shell, they do not.

+<details>

+</details>

 ## Learning objectives
-- Navigate the Linux directory tree from `/`.
-- Explain the purpose of major system directories.

+- Navigate the Linux directory tree from `/` with confidence.
+- Explain the purpose of every major top-level directory.
+- Know where configuration, logs, and variable data live before you ever need them in an incident.
 
 ## Introduction
-Linux presents filesystems through one directory tree. The top of that tree is `/`, called the root directory. Separate disks and partitions are mounted at directories within this tree.
+Linux presents all storage through **one directory tree**. The top of that tree is `/`, the root directory. Additional disks, partitions, network shares, and even virtual kernel filesystems are **mounted** at directories inside this single tree, so unlike Windows there are no drive letters: your second disk might simply appear as `/mnt/data`.
+
+Learning this map early pays off constantly. When a service fails, its configuration is in `/etc` and its logs are in `/var/log`. When a disk fills, the answer is almost always somewhere under `/var` or `/home`. You will use this lesson's table for the rest of your career.

+Three of these deserve special respect:
 
-### Paths
-- Absolute path starts at `/`, e.g. `/etc/ssh/sshd_config`.
-- Relative path starts from the current working directory, e.g. `notes/todo.txt`.
-- `.` means current directory; `..` means parent directory; `~` expands to the current user's home directory in common shells.
+- **`/etc`** holds the configuration that defines how this particular machine behaves: users (`/etc/passwd`), network settings, SSH policy (`/etc/ssh/sshd_config`), and service definitions. Back up a file before editing it, and edit deliberately.
+- **`/var/log`** is where the system tells you what happened: authentication attempts (`auth.log` or the journal), package history, service errors. During any investigation, this is your first stop.
+- **`/proc` and `/sys`** are **virtual filesystems**. Their entries are generated by the kernel on the fly and represent live kernel state, not stored files. Reading them is safe and informative (`cat /proc/cpuinfo`); writing to them changes a running kernel, so do not edit them casually.
 
 ### Configuration and logs
-Common locations include `/etc/` for configuration, `/var/log/` for many logs, and service-specific directories under `/var/lib/`. Exact files depend on distribution and service. Use `journalctl` for systemd journal logs where applicable.
+The pattern to memorize: configuration in `/etc/`, logs in `/var/log/`, service data under `/var/lib/`. Exact files depend on distribution and service. On systemd systems, much logging lives in the journal rather than plain files; `journalctl` (module 07) is how you read it.

+-rw-r--r-- 1 root root 8 Nov 20 12:00 /etc/hostname
+```
+
+`/proc/cpuinfo` reports size 0 yet prints pages of content when read: the kernel generates it on demand. The size field is meaningless for virtual files.

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** What is the difference between `/` and `/root`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`/` is the root of the entire directory tree. `/root` is the home directory of the root user, one directory inside that tree. The shared word "root" causes endless confusion; the two are unrelated concepts.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** A colleague cannot find the second disk they attached, because "there is no D: drive". Where should they look, and why?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Anywhere in the single tree where it was mounted: conventionally under `/mnt` or `/media`, or wherever the administrator chose. Linux mounts every filesystem into the one tree under `/`; there are no drive letters.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** A web application fails to start. Which two directories do you check first, and for what?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`/etc` for the service's configuration (wrong port, wrong path, bad syntax) and `/var/log` (or `journalctl`) for the error it logged when it tried to start.
+</details>

 ## Learning objectives
-- Recognize hidden files and common file types.
-- Explain extensions, inodes, hard links, and symbolic links.
-- Inspect file metadata safely.
+- Recognize hidden files and common file types, and explain what extensions do and do not mean.
+- Explain inodes, hard links, and symbolic links well enough to predict their behavior.
+- Inspect file metadata safely with `ls`, `file`, and `stat`.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A filename beginning with `.` is hidden by convention; it is not encrypted or protected. Examples include `.bashrc` and `.ssh/`. Linux does not require extensions: a file named `report` may contain text, while a `.jpg` file may not actually be an image.
 
-The filesystem stores metadata in an **inode**: ownership, permissions, timestamps, and pointers to file data (implementation details vary). A directory maps names to inode numbers.
+### Hidden files
+A filename beginning with `.` is hidden **by convention**: `ls` and graphical file managers simply do not show it unless asked (`ls -a`). Hidden does not mean encrypted, protected, or secure. Anyone with read permission can see a hidden file with `ls -a`. Your home directory is full of them because they hold configuration: `.bashrc`, `.profile`, `.ssh/`, `.gitconfig`.
 
-A **hard link** is another directory entry referring to the same inode. It generally cannot cross filesystem boundaries and normally cannot be made to directories by ordinary users. A **symbolic link** is a special file containing a path to another object; it can cross filesystems and can become dangling if its target moves.
+> [!WARNING]
+> Never treat a dotfile as a secret store. `.ssh/` contains private keys, but they are protected by *permissions* (module 06), not by the dot in the name.
+
+### Extensions and real file types
+Linux does not require extensions, and the kernel does not care about them. A file named `report` may be plain text, and a file named `photo.jpg` may be a script. Extensions are hints for humans and for some applications, nothing more. To learn what a file actually contains, ask the filesystem to inspect its content:
 
-## Commands
 ```bash
-ls -la
 file /etc/passwd
-stat /etc/passwd
-ln report.txt report-hardlink.txt
-ln -s report.txt report-symlink.txt
-ls -li report*
+file photo.jpg
 ```
-`ls -i` displays inode numbers. `stat` shows detailed metadata. `file` identifies content using signatures and heuristics rather than trusting the extension.
 
-Create a safe demonstration:
+Sample output:
+
+```text
+/etc/passwd: ASCII text
+photo.jpg:   JPEG image data, JFIF standard 1.01
+```
+
+`file` reads the first bytes and matches them against a database of signatures ("magic numbers"), so it reports the real type regardless of the name.
+
+### Inodes
+The filesystem stores a file's metadata in an **inode**: ownership, permissions, timestamps, size, and pointers to the actual data blocks. The inode does *not* store the filename. A directory is a table mapping **names to inode numbers**, which is why one file's data can have several names, and why a name can point at nothing.
+
+See the inode numbers directly:
+
 ```bash
-mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/links
-cd ~/linux-lab/links
-printf 'sample
-' > original.txt
+ls -li report.txt
+stat report.txt
+```
+
+`ls -i` adds the inode column; `stat` prints the full metadata record: size, permissions, owner, inode number, link count, and the three timestamps (access, modify, change).
+
+### Hard links and symbolic links
+A **hard link** is a second directory entry that names the *same inode*. The two names are equal citizens: neither is "the original". The data survives until the last name is removed. Hard links generally cannot cross filesystem boundaries, and ordinary users cannot make them to directories.
+
+A **symbolic link** (symlink) is a small special file that *contains a path* to another object. It can cross filesystems and point to directories, but it is only a pointer: if the target moves or is deleted, the symlink dangles and breaks.
+
+Create a safe demonstration and watch the difference:
+
+```bash
+mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/links && cd ~/linux-lab/links
+printf 'sample\n' > original.txt
 ln original.txt hard.txt
 ln -s original.txt symbolic.txt
-stat original.txt hard.txt symbolic.txt
+ls -li
+```
+
+Sample output (inode numbers will differ):
+
+```text
+1049090 -rw-rw-r-- 2 learner learner 7 Nov 25 04:15 hard.txt
+1049090 -rw-rw-r-- 2 learner learner 7 Nov 25 04:15 original.txt
+1049091 lrwxrwxrwx 1 learner learner 12 Nov 25 04:15 symbolic.txt -> original.txt
+```
+
+`hard.txt` and `original.txt` share inode `1049090`, and the link count is `2`. The symlink has its own inode and a type letter `l`, with its target shown after `->`. Now rename the original:
+
+```bash
+mv original.txt renamed.txt
+cat hard.txt        # works: same inode
+cat symbolic.txt    # fails: points at the old path
+```
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Create one hidden file and one extensionless text file in `~/linux-lab/links`, then use `ls -la`, `file`, and `stat` to inspect both.

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+cd ~/linux-lab/links
+printf 'data\n' > base.txt
+ln base.txt base-hard.txt
+ln -s base.txt base-sym.txt
+rm base.txt
+cat base-hard.txt   # still works
+cat base-sym.txt    # No such file or directory
+ls -l base-sym.txt
 ```
-The original and hard link share an inode; the symlink has its own inode and points to the target.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Create one hidden file and one extensionless text file.
-- Use `ls -la`, `file`, `stat`, and `ls -li` to inspect them.
-- Create a hard link and a symbolic link; rename the original and observe which link still resolves.
-- Hint: a symlink stores a path, while a hard link names the same inode.
+The hard link keeps the inode (and its data) alive. The symlink now dangles; many `ls` configurations highlight broken symlinks in a warning color.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** A download is named `setup.bin` but you suspect it is actually a shell script. Identify it without executing it, then read the first lines safely.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+file setup.bin
+head -n 5 setup.bin
+```
+
+`file` will say something like `Bourne-Again shell script, ASCII text executable` if it is a script. `head` shows the shebang line (`#!/bin/bash`) without running anything. Never execute an unidentified file to "see what it does".
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Is a file named `.secret` protected from other users? Why or why not?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. The leading dot only hides it from default listings. Anyone with read permission on the file (and search permission on its directory) can read it with `ls -a` and `cat`. Protection comes from permissions, not names.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** You rename `original.txt` to `renamed.txt`. What happens to a hard link and to a symlink that both pointed at `original.txt`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The hard link still works: it names the inode, which never changed. The symlink breaks: it stores the old path, which no longer exists.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** What does the link count in `ls -l` (the number after the permissions) mean for a regular file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The number of directory entries (names) pointing to that inode. A plain file starts at 1; each hard link adds one. The data is freed only when the count reaches 0 and no process holds the file open.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Assuming hidden means secure.
-- Assuming extensions determine file type.
-- Deleting a symlink's target when intending to remove only the link. `rm linkname` removes the link itself, not the target.
+- Assuming the extension determines the type; check with `file` instead.
+- Confusing link removal semantics: `rm linkname` removes the link itself, not its target, but careless use of tab completion or wildcards around symlinks to directories can still bite.
+- Believing a symlink protects data when the target is deleted; the symlink survives, the data does not.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Names, extensions, file types, and inodes are different concepts. Hard links share an inode; symbolic links point by path.
+Names, extensions, file types, and inodes are four different concepts. A hard link is another name for the same inode; a symlink is a path in a file. `file` and `stat` tell you the truth about both.
 
 ## Further practice
-Read `man ln`, `man stat`, and `man inode`.
+Read `man ln`, `man stat`, and `man inode`, then hunt for real symlinks on your system: `ls -l /bin /usr/bin | head` on a merged-usr system shows `/bin` itself as a symlink.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain how a command is parsed and executed.
-- Use built-in help and manual pages.
-- Understand quoting, escaping, globbing, and environment variables.
+- Explain what the terminal, the shell, and a command really are, and how a command is parsed and executed.
+- Use built-in help, manual pages, and `type` to understand any command you meet.
+- Control quoting, escaping, globbing, and environment variables so commands do exactly what you intend.
 
-## Core concepts
-A command commonly has the form `command [options] [arguments]`. Options change behavior; arguments identify targets or values. Commands may be external programs or shell built-ins.
+## Terminal, shell, and command: three different things
+People say "the terminal" for everything, but three separate pieces are involved every time you run a command:
 
-The shell expands variables, quotes, wildcards, and command substitutions before starting an external program. This is why quoting matters.
+- The **terminal** (or terminal emulator) is the window on your screen. It draws text and sends your keystrokes to the shell. GNOME Terminal, Konsole, Windows Terminal, and the Linux virtual consoles are all terminals.
+- The **shell** is the program running inside that window. It reads the line you typed, interprets it, starts programs, and prints their output. On most Linux systems the shell is **Bash**; others include Zsh and Dash.
+- A **command** is one instruction you give the shell: a program name plus optional modifiers.
 
-### Quoting and special characters
-- `'single quotes'` preserve literal characters (no variable expansion).
-- `"double quotes"` allow variable and command substitution but prevent word splitting and pathname expansion in the quoted text.
-- `\` escapes the next character in many contexts.
-- `*` matches any string; `?` matches one character; `[abc]` matches one listed character.
-- `~` commonly expands to the home directory.
-- `$NAME` expands an environment variable.
-- `$(command)` runs a command and substitutes its output.
+You can check which shell you are running:
 
-Examples:
 ```bash
-name='Linux learner'
-printf '%s
-' "$name"
-printf '%s
-' '$name'
-printf 'Home: %s
-' "$HOME"
-kernel="$(uname -r)"
-printf 'Kernel: %s
-' "$kernel"
-```
-
-### Help
+echo "$SHELL"
+printf '%s\n' "$0"
+```
+
+Typical output:
+
+```text
+/bin/bash
+bash
+```
+
+This distinction matters because some behavior belongs to the shell (history, tab completion, wildcards), some to the terminal (copy/paste shortcuts, scrollback), and some to the command itself (its options and output). When something behaves unexpectedly, knowing which layer owns the behavior tells you where to look.
+
+## Anatomy of a command
+A command line commonly has the form `command [options] [arguments]`:
+
+```bash
+ls -lah /var/log
+```
+
+- `ls` is the **command**: the program to run.
+- `-lah` is a bundle of three **options** (`-l`, `-a`, `-h`). Options change behavior. Short options start with one dash and can usually be bundled; long options start with two dashes and stand alone (`--all`). Some options take a value of their own, as in `grep -n 3 file` or `cut -d ,`.
+- `/var/log` is an **argument**: the target the command acts on. Many commands accept several arguments, or none.
+
+Commands come in two kinds. **External programs** are files on disk, usually in directories like `/bin` and `/usr/bin`. **Shell built-ins** are features of the shell itself: `cd`, `echo`, `pwd`, `history`, and `export` are built-ins in Bash. The difference shows up when you look for help (see below) and when you script.
+
+## What the shell does before your command runs
+The most important idea in this lesson: **the shell rewrites your line before the command ever sees it.** This rewriting is called *expansion*, and it happens in a fixed order. The command receives only the final, expanded words.
+
+Watch it happen with `echo`, which simply prints the arguments it receives:
+
+```bash
+echo home is $HOME
+echo files: *.conf
+echo today is $(date +%F)
+echo "literal: $HOME *.conf $(date)"
+```
+
+Sample output (yours will differ):
+
+```text
+home is /home/learner
+files: app.conf db.conf
+today is 2026-11-25
+literal: $HOME *.conf $(date)
+```
+
+In order, the first three lines performed **variable expansion** (`$HOME`), **pathname expansion** (`*.conf` became the matching filenames in the current directory), and **command substitution** (`$(date +%F)` ran `date` and inserted its output). The fourth line was quoted, so nothing expanded.
+
+Other expansions you will meet early:
+
+- `~` expands to your home directory; `~alice` expands to user alice's home.
+- `{a,b,c}` is **brace expansion**: `mkdir lab_{one,two}` creates `lab_one` and `lab_two`.
+- `$((2 + 3))` is arithmetic expansion.
+
+### Quoting and escaping
+Quoting controls expansion:
+
+- `'single quotes'` preserve everything literally. Nothing expands, ever.
+- `"double quotes"` allow `$variable`, `$(command)`, and backslash escapes, but prevent word splitting and pathname expansion.
+- A backslash `\` escapes the single character that follows it.
+
+Word splitting is the reason quoting is not optional. After expansion, the shell splits the line into separate words at unquoted spaces. Compare:
+
+```bash
+mkdir "my lab"
+mkdir my lab
+```
+
+The first creates one directory named `my lab`. The second creates two directories, `my` and `lab`, because the unquoted space split the argument in two. The same rule applies to variables:
+
+```bash
+name='linux lab'
+printf '%s\n' "$name"   # one argument: linux lab
+printf '%s\n' $name     # two arguments: linux and lab
+```
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> Unquoted variables are one of the most common causes of destructive scripts. `rm -r $dir/*` with an empty or space-filled `dir` can delete things you never intended. Quote every variable unless you specifically want splitting.
+
+### Globbing patterns
+When a word contains an unquoted wildcard, the shell replaces it with matching filenames:
+
+- `*` matches any string, including empty: `ls *.log`.
+- `?` matches exactly one character: `ls data?.csv`.
+- `[abc]` matches one listed character; `[0-9]` matches a range: `ls report[0-9].txt`.
+- `[!abc]` matches one character *not* listed.
+
+If nothing matches, Bash passes the pattern through literally, which confuses many commands. And if the pattern would match something dangerous, think before combining it with `rm`.
+
+### Environment variables
+Variables live in your shell session. Some are marked for **export**, which means child processes inherit a copy; those are environment variables. Key ones:
+
+- `HOME`: your home directory.
+- `PATH`: the colon-separated list of directories the shell searches for commands.
+- `USER`, `SHELL`, `PWD`, `LANG`.
+
+```bash
+printf '%s\n' "$PATH"
+project='linux lab'        # shell variable, current shell only
+export project             # now child processes see it too
+env | grep '^project='
+```
+
+Assignments without `export` disappear when the shell exits and are invisible to programs you start. Permanent variables are set in startup files such as `~/.bashrc`.
+
+## Getting help inside the terminal
+You rarely need the web to learn a command. Linux ships its documentation with the system:
+
 ```bash
-man ls
-ls --help
-help cd
-type cd
+man ls            # full manual page; press q to quit, / to search
+ls --help         # short option summary from the command itself
+help cd           # documentation for a Bash built-in
+type cd           # how the shell resolves this name
 type ls
-which python3
-history
-clear
-```
-`history` displays the current shell's command history (Bash commonly keeps a bounded history in memory and may save it to `~/.bash_history`). `clear` clears the visible terminal display; it does not delete command history or files.
-`man` opens a manual page; press `q` to quit. `help` documents Bash built-ins. `type` identifies how the current shell resolves a name. `which` searches `PATH` for an executable and may not identify aliases or built-ins reliably.
-
-## Efficiency
-- Tab completes commands and paths.
-- Up/Down navigate history; `Ctrl-R` searches history in Bash.
-- `Ctrl-A` and `Ctrl-E` move to line start/end.
-- `Ctrl-U` clears from cursor to line start; `Ctrl-K` clears to line end.
-- `Ctrl-C` interrupts the foreground command; `Ctrl-D` sends end-of-input (often exits an interactive shell).
-- `history` lists recent commands. Avoid placing secrets in commands because history may retain them.
-
-## Hands-on practice
-1. Create a directory whose name contains spaces and list it using correct quoting.
-2. Set `project='linux lab'` and print it safely.
-3. Use `type` on `cd`, `ls`, and `grep`; compare results.
-4. Open `man grep`, search for `-i`, and quit.
-5. Hint: quote a complete path containing spaces.
+which python3     # which file PATH resolves to this name
+apropos copy      # search man page summaries for a keyword
+```
+
+- `man` pages are organized into numbered sections. Section 1 is user commands, section 5 is file formats, section 8 is administration commands. Occasionally you need the section number: `man 5 passwd` documents the `/etc/passwd` file format, while `man 1 passwd` documents the password-changing command.
+- `help` only covers built-ins. `man bash` covers the shell itself, including every expansion rule in this lesson.
+- `type` is the reliable way to ask "what will actually run?": it reports built-ins, aliases, functions, and external paths. `which` only searches `PATH` and can mislead you about aliases and built-ins.
+- `history` lists recent commands from the current shell. Bash keeps a bounded in-memory history and commonly saves it to `~/.bash_history` on exit.
+
+> [!IMPORTANT]
+> Never type passwords or tokens directly into a command line. They land in your shell history and are visible in process listings while the command runs.
+
+## Efficiency essentials
+The shell rewards a handful of keystrokes:
+
+- **Tab** completes commands, paths, and filenames. Press it twice to list possibilities.
+- **Up / Down** walk through history; **Ctrl-R** searches it interactively.
+- **Ctrl-A** / **Ctrl-E** jump to the start / end of the line.
+- **Ctrl-U** deletes from the cursor to the start; **Ctrl-K** deletes to the end.
+- **Ctrl-C** interrupts the foreground command; **Ctrl-D** sends end-of-input (an empty line at a shell prompt usually logs you out).
+- **Ctrl-L** clears the screen, like the `clear` command. Neither touches history or files.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Create a directory named exactly `course notes` (with the space) in your home directory, then list it with `ls -ld`.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+mkdir "$HOME/course notes"
+ls -ld "$HOME/course notes"
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+drwxrwxr-x 2 learner learner 4096 Nov 25 04:10 /home/learner/course notes
+```
+
+The quotes keep the space inside one argument. Without them you would create two directories.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Set a variable `project='linux lab'` and print it on its own line. Then print the literal text `$project` (the dollar sign included).
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+project='linux lab'
+printf '%s\n' "$project"
+printf '%s\n' '$project'
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+linux lab
+$project
+```
+
+Double quotes expand the variable; single quotes preserve it literally.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Run `type` on `cd`, `ls`, `grep`, and `history`. Which are built-ins and which are external programs? For each external one, where does the file live?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+type cd ls grep history
+```
+
+Typical output:
+
+```text
+cd is a shell builtin
+ls is aliased to `ls --color=auto'
+grep is /usr/bin/grep
+history is a shell builtin
+```
+
+`cd` and `history` are built-ins. `ls` is often an alias wrapping `/usr/bin/ls`; `grep` is a plain external program.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** Open `man grep`, find the documentation for the `-i` option using `/` search, then quit. What does `-i` do?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Run `man grep`, type `/-i` and press Enter, press `n` to jump between matches, and `q` to quit. `-i` makes matching case-insensitive (`--ignore-case`).
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 5.** Using only globbing (no `find`), list every file in `/etc` whose name ends in `.conf`, then every file in `/var/log` whose name contains a digit.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls /etc/*.conf
+ls /var/log/*[0-9]*
+```
+
+The second pattern uses a bracket range, which matches any single digit anywhere in the name.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** You run `echo $UNSAVED` and get an empty line, but you typed `UNSAVED=hello` a minute ago in the same shell. What is the most likely explanation?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The variable was never assigned in *this* shell, or the shell was restarted. Assignments do not survive a closed terminal. (If it were assigned but not exported, `echo` would still print it, because `echo` runs in the current shell.)
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** `rm report final.txt` deleted a file called `report` but not your file `report final.txt`. Why, and how do you remove the right file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+The unquoted space split the name into two arguments. Remove it with quoting: `rm "report final.txt"` or `rm report\ final.txt`.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Which single command tells you whether `python3` is a built-in, an alias, or an external program, and where it lives?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`type python3` (`type -a python3` shows every match in resolution order).
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Forgetting quotes around variables that may contain spaces.
-- Assuming `which` describes aliases and shell functions.
-- Pasting commands without understanding shell metacharacters.
-- Typing passwords directly into command arguments.
+- Forgetting quotes around variables or filenames that contain spaces.
+- Assuming `which` describes aliases and shell functions; use `type` instead.
+- Pasting commands from the web without understanding the metacharacters in them.
+- Typing passwords directly into command arguments, where history and process lists keep them.
+- Expecting `unset`-style changes or plain assignments to reach other programs without `export`.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-The shell performs expansions before execution. Quoting is essential for predictable and safe commands.
+The shell expands variables, globs, and substitutions **before** running anything, and the command only sees the expanded result. Quoting is how you stay in control of that rewriting, and `type` plus `man` are how you stay in control of unfamiliar commands.

-Read `man bash` sections on quoting, expansions, and history.
+Read the `man bash` sections on quoting, expansions, and history. Then spend one day doing every file task you would normally do in a file manager from the shell instead.

@@ -2,49 +2,217 @@
 
 
 ## Learning objectives
-- Navigate with `pwd`, `cd`, and `ls`.
-- Use relative and absolute paths.
-- Use history, completion, and directory shortcuts efficiently.
+- Navigate the filesystem confidently with `pwd`, `cd`, and `ls`.
+- Choose between absolute and relative paths, and predict what each resolves to.
+- Read a long `ls` listing field by field.
+- Move fast with history, completion, and directory shortcuts.
+
+## Where am I: the working directory
+Every process, including your shell, has a **current working directory**. Relative paths are resolved against it, which is why the same command can do different things in different places. Your first habit: confirm your location before and after moving.
+
+```bash
+pwd
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+/home/learner
+```
+
+## Absolute and relative paths
+An **absolute path** starts at the filesystem root `/` and works from anywhere: `/etc/ssh/sshd_config` means the same thing no matter where you stand.
+
+A **relative path** starts from your current directory. If `pwd` is `/home/learner`, then `docs/notes.txt` means `/home/learner/docs/notes.txt`. Three building blocks make relative paths expressive:
+
+- `.` is the current directory. `./script.sh` runs a script in the current directory (deliberately *not* searched by `PATH`).
+- `..` is the parent directory. From `/home/learner/docs`, `..` is `/home/learner`, and `../Pictures` is `/home/learner/Pictures`.
+- `~` is your home directory, expanded by the shell before the command runs.
+
+Compare:
+
+```bash
+cd /tmp        # absolute: /tmp, from anywhere
+cd tmp         # relative: the "tmp" inside your current directory, if it exists
+cd ..          # up one level
+cd ../..       # up two levels
+cd ~           # home
+cd -           # back to the previous directory (toggle)
+cd             # bare cd also goes home
+```
+
+`cd -` is worth learning early: it jumps back to wherever you were before the last `cd`, and prints that path. Two `cd -` in a row toggle you between two directories, which is ideal for comparing or copying between them.
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> `cd /tmp` and `cd tmp` are not the same command. Mixing up absolute and relative paths is how files get created in the wrong place, and how `rm -r` hits the wrong tree.
+
+## Listing directories: ls in depth
+`ls` shows what a path contains. The options you will use daily:
+
+```bash
+ls            # names only
+ls -l         # long format: permissions, owner, size, time
+ls -a         # include hidden entries (names starting with a dot)
+ls -A         # hidden entries except "." and ".."
+ls -h         # with -l, human-readable sizes (K, M, G)
+ls -t         # sort by modification time, newest first
+ls -r         # reverse the sort order
+ls -lah /var/log   # the classic bundle
+```
+
+A long listing line rewards careful reading:
+
+```text
+drwxr-xr-x 3 root root 4096 Nov 25 03:58 ssh
+```
+
+Field by field: `d` says this is a directory (`-` for a regular file, `l` for a symlink); `rwxr-xr-x` is the permission set you will study in module 06; `3` is the link count; then owner, group, size in bytes, last modification time, and the name.
+
+Two views of the bigger picture:
 
-## Commands
 ```bash
+tree -L 2 ~/linux-lab
+find /etc -maxdepth 1 -type d
+```
+
+`tree` draws a hierarchy (`-L` limits depth) but may need installation. `find` is always available; you will learn it properly in module 10.
+
+Hidden files matter more than beginners expect. Configuration for your whole environment lives in dotfiles like `~/.bashrc` and `~/.ssh/`. Plain `ls` hides them; `ls -a` is how you see the real contents of a home directory.
+
+## Shell efficiency: doing less typing
+Everything from lesson 01 applies here; these habits pay off specifically while navigating:
+
+- **Tab completion** is your spell-checker for paths. Type `cd /etc/ss` + Tab and the shell completes `ssh/`. Press Tab twice to list candidates when the prefix is ambiguous. If nothing completes, the path probably does not exist.
+- **History search**: `Ctrl-R`, then type part of an old command, Enter to run it or the arrow keys to edit it first. Far safer than retyping a long path.
+- **Line editing**: `Ctrl-A` / `Ctrl-E` to the line start/end, `Ctrl-U` to delete backwards, `Ctrl-K` to delete forwards. Fixing a typo at the start of a long command takes one keystroke, not twenty.
+- **Reuse arguments**: `!$` expands to the last argument of the previous command. `mkdir project && cd !$` creates a directory and enters it.
+
+```bash
+mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/notes && cd !$
 pwd
-ls
-ls -lah
+history | tail -n 5
+```
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Starting anywhere, go to `/etc` with an absolute path, then to its parent with a relative path, then home, then back to where you just were, in four commands total.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
 cd /etc
 cd ..
-cd ~
+cd
 cd -
 ```
-- `pwd`: print current directory.
-- `ls -l`: long listing; `-a`: include hidden entries; `-h`: human-readable sizes; `-A`: include hidden entries except `.` and `..`.
-- `cd`: change directory; `cd -` returns to the previous directory; `cd ~` goes home.
 
-`tree` displays a directory hierarchy but may need installation:
+`cd /etc` is absolute; `cd ..` moves to `/`; bare `cd` goes home; `cd -` returns to `/`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Create the path `~/linux-lab/deep/nested/dir` with a single command, enter it, then return to `~/linux-lab` without typing the words `linux-lab` again.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
 ```bash
-tree -L 2 ~/linux-lab
+mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/deep/nested/dir
+cd ~/linux-lab/deep/nested/dir
+cd ../../..
+pwd
+```

+Output:
+
+```text
+/home/learner/linux-lab
 ```
-`-L` limits depth. If unavailable, use `find`.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Starting in your home directory, navigate to `/etc` using an absolute path.
-- Move to its parent using `cd ..`.
-- Return home with `cd`.
-- Use `cd -` twice and explain what changed.
-- List hidden entries in your home directory.
-- Hint: `pwd` confirms your location after every navigation step.
+Each `..` climbs one level: `nested`, `deep`, `linux-lab`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** List all entries in your home directory including hidden ones, in long format with human-readable sizes, sorted so the most recently changed appear last.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls -lahrt ~
+```
+
+`-t` sorts newest first; `-r` reverses it so the newest end up at the bottom, nearest your prompt.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** In the `ls -l` line below, what is the owner's name, is this a file or a directory, and what is its size?
+
+```text
+-rw-r----- 1 learner labteam 1280 Nov 24 22:41 notes.txt
+```
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Owner `learner` (group `labteam`), a regular file (leading `-`), 1280 bytes.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 5.** Using history and editing keys only: re-run the last `ls` command you typed, but change its target to `/var/log` before running it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Press `Ctrl-R`, type `ls`, press the Right arrow (or `Ctrl-E`) to load the line for editing instead of running it, edit the path to `/var/log` using `Ctrl-A` / arrows and `Ctrl-K` / `Ctrl-U` to trim, then press Enter.
+</details>

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** You are in `/home/learner/docs` and run `cd Pictures`. The shell reports "No such file or directory", yet `~/Pictures` exists. What went wrong?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`cd Pictures` is relative: it looks for `/home/learner/docs/Pictures`, which does not exist. Use `cd ~/Pictures` (absolute via home) or `cd ../Pictures`.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What is the difference between `ls -a` and `ls -A`, and why does the difference matter when scripting?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`ls -a` includes `.` and `..` themselves; `ls -A` shows hidden files but excludes them. In a loop like `for f in *` or when parsing `ls -a`, the `.` and `..` entries can make a script act on the current and parent directories unexpectedly.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** After `mkdir reports && cd reports`, which single shortcut takes you back out without typing a path?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`cd -` (it returns to the previous working directory). `cd ..` also works here, but `cd -` works no matter where you came from.
+</details>
+
+**Q4.** Why does `./backup.sh` work when `backup.sh` alone gives "command not found", even while you are standing in the script's directory?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+For safety, the current directory is not in `PATH`, so the shell never searches it. `./backup.sh` is an explicit relative path to the file, bypassing the `PATH` search.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Confusing `cd /tmp` with `cd tmp`.
-- Forgetting that relative paths depend on the current directory.
-- Assuming `ls` lists hidden files by default.
+- Confusing `cd /tmp` with `cd tmp`; check `pwd` when in doubt.
+- Forgetting that relative paths depend on the current directory, especially inside scripts.
+- Assuming `ls` shows hidden files; it does not.
+- Retyping long commands instead of using `Ctrl-R`, Tab, and `!$`.
+- Running `./something` from an untrusted directory just because it exists; executing local files is an explicit action for a reason.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Use `pwd` to verify context, and prefer explicit paths when a command could affect important files.
+`pwd` tells you the truth about where you are; absolute paths are unambiguous and relative paths are short. Read `ls -l` field by field, and let Tab, history search, and `cd -` carry the typing for you.
 
 ## Further practice
-Practice moving between `/`, `/etc`, `/var/log`, and your home directory without using a graphical file manager.
+Spend one session moving only between `/`, `/etc`, `/var/log`, `/tmp`, and your home directory without a graphical file manager, using each shortcut from this lesson at least once. Then explore `man 7 glob` for the full pattern language you met in lesson 01.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Create files and directories.
-- Copy and move files safely.
-- Explain the risks of recursive deletion.
+- Create, copy, move, and remove files and directories deliberately.
+- Choose the right options for each operation and predict the result before pressing Enter.
+- Explain the risks of recursive deletion and apply a safe-deletion routine every time.
 
 ## Commands
+
 ```bash
 mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/{input,backup}
 touch ~/linux-lab/input/notes.txt
@@ -14,56 +15,158 @@ cp ~/linux-lab/input/notes.txt ~/linux-lab/backup/
 mv ~/linux-lab/backup/notes.txt ~/linux-lab/backup/notes-old.txt
 rmdir ~/linux-lab/empty-dir
 ```
-- `mkdir -p`: create parent directories as needed; do not fail if the target already exists.
-- `touch`: create an empty file if absent or update timestamps if present.
-- `cp`: copy; `-r` recursively copies directories; `-i` prompts before overwrite; `-a` preserves attributes as far as possible.
-- `mv`: move or rename; `-i` prompts before overwrite.
-- `rmdir`: removes empty directories only.
-- `rm`: removes files; `-r` recursively removes directory trees; `-f` suppresses prompts/errors for missing files.
-
-### Safe deletion
-Before deleting:
+
+What each command really does:
+
+- `mkdir -p`: creates parent directories as needed and does not fail if the target already exists. Without `-p`, `mkdir a/b/c` fails if `a/b` does not exist. The `{input,backup}` brace expansion creates both directories in one command.
+- `touch`: creates an empty file if it is absent, or **updates the timestamps** of an existing file without changing its content. It is not a "make file" command so much as a "make or freshen" command.
+- `cp`: copies. Useful options: `-r` copies directories recursively (required, or `cp` refuses directories), `-i` prompts before overwriting, `-a` archives, preserving permissions, timestamps, and links as far as possible. `cp file dir/` places a copy inside `dir`; `cp file newname` copies to a new name in place.
+- `mv`: moves or renames. Same filesystem or not, the *name* changes; across filesystems the data is actually copied and the original removed. `-i` prompts before overwriting.
+- `rmdir`: removes **empty** directories only. Its refusal to remove anything non-empty makes it the safe choice.
+- `rm`: removes files. `-r` descends into directory trees; `-f` suppresses prompts and missing-file errors. `rm` does not move anything to a trash can: deletion is immediate and effectively permanent.
+
+### Safe deletion routine
+Before any deletion that matters:
+
 1. Print the current directory with `pwd`.
 2. Inspect the exact target with `ls -la -- target`.
-3. Prefer a narrow path and interactive mode (`rm -i`) while learning.
-4. Avoid `rm -rf` with variables, wildcards, or paths you have not verified.
-5. Never copy a command containing `sudo rm -rf` from an untrusted source.
+3. Prefer a narrow, fully spelled-out path and interactive mode (`rm -i`) while learning.
+4. Avoid `rm -rf` combined with variables, wildcards, or paths you have not just verified.
+5. Never paste a command containing `sudo rm -rf` from an untrusted source.
 
 Example:
+
 ```bash
 rm -i ~/linux-lab/input/old.txt
 ```
-Do not test destructive commands on real data.
 
-## Inspecting files
+Output:
+
+```text
+rm: remove regular empty file '/home/learner/linux-lab/input/old.txt'? y
+```
+
+> [!CAUTION]
+> The classic disaster is a variable or space in the wrong place: `rm -rf $dir/*` with an empty `dir` becomes `rm -rf /*`. Quote variables, verify paths, and test destructive commands on lab data only.

+## Inspecting what you created
 ```bash

+```
+
+`file` identifies content, `stat` shows full metadata, and that `find` one-liner lists every file under your lab to two levels deep. Run it after each exercise to confirm the tree looks the way you think it does.

+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** Without running it, say exactly what this command does, and name one reason it is dangerous in a script:
+
+```bash

+```
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+It recursively and forcefully deletes the `tmp` subdirectory of whatever `$workdir` expands to. If `$workdir` is empty or unset, the path becomes `/tmp`, deleting the system temp directory; if it contains spaces and is unquoted, the command may delete several unintended paths. Quote it (`"$workdir/tmp"`), verify the variable, and consider `rm -ri` during testing.
+</details>

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** You run `touch existing.txt` on a file full of important data. Did you just erase it?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. `touch` only updates the file's timestamps; the content is untouched. It creates a file only when the file does not already exist.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Why does `cp mydir /tmp/` fail when `mydir` is a directory, and what is the fix?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`cp` refuses directories without `-r` ("omitting directory"). Use `cp -r mydir /tmp/`, or `cp -a` to also preserve attributes.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Is `rm -i` a real safety mechanism or just a comfort feature?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+A real one for interactive mistakes: it forces a per-file confirmation, which catches wrong paths, wrong directories, and over-broad wildcards before damage. It does nothing for you inside scripts, where the routine (verify `pwd`, narrow paths, quote variables) is the protection.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Using `rmdir` on a non-empty directory.
+- Using `rmdir` on a non-empty directory and being surprised by the refusal.
 - Forgetting `cp -r` for a directory.
-- Running `rm -rf` from an unexpected working directory.
-- Assuming `touch` always creates a new file; it updates an existing file's timestamp.
+- Running recursive `rm` from an unexpected working directory; `pwd` first.
+- Assuming `touch` always creates a new file.
+- Believing deleted files go somewhere recoverable; `rm` is final.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Inspect targets before changing them. `rmdir` is safer for empty directories; recursive `rm` deserves extra caution.
+Inspect targets before changing them, quote variables, and treat recursive `rm` with the respect you would give a loaded tool. `rmdir` for empty directories, `rm -i` while learning, and `ls` after every operation.

-Read `man cp`, `man mv`, and `man rm`.
+Read `man cp`, `man mv`, and `man rm`. Pay attention to `-n` (no clobber) as the opposite of `-f`, and decide which default behavior you want in your own aliases later.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Read text files using suitable tools.
-- Inspect the beginning or end of large files.
-- Understand why binary files should not be dumped blindly.
+- Choose the right tool to read a file based on its size and your purpose.
+- Inspect the beginning or end of large files, and follow growing logs live.

+
+## The right reader for the job
 
-## Commands
 ```bash
 cat file.txt
 less file.txt
@@ -16,26 +17,148 @@ tail -f application.log
 file unknown.bin
 stat unknown.bin
 ```
-`cat` concatenates files to standard output and is best for short files. `less` supports paging and searching (`/pattern`, `n`, `N`, `q`). `head` and `tail` show selected ends. `tail -f` follows a changing file; `Ctrl-C` stops following.
 
-Use `file` before assuming a file is text. Printing arbitrary binary data with `cat` can produce terminal control characters and unreadable output.
+- `cat` writes whole files to standard output. Perfect for short files and for feeding other commands; painful for anything longer than a screen, because the beginning scrolls away before you can read it.
+- `less` is the pager: open, scroll, and search without loading everything at once. Essential keys: `/pattern` to search forward, `n` / `N` for next / previous match, `g` / `G` for top / bottom, `q` to quit.
+- `head` and `tail` show the first or last lines; `-n` sets how many. Most real questions about a file are answered by one of its ends.
+- `tail -f` follows a file as it grows, printing new lines as they arrive. This is how you watch a log while reproducing a problem. `Ctrl-C` stops following.
+
+Sample session:
+
+```text
+$ tail -n 3 /var/log/dpkg.log
+2026-11-25 03:58:12 status installed bash:amd64 5.2.21-2ubuntu4
+2026-11-25 03:58:13 trigproc libc-bin:amd64 2.39-0ubuntu8
+2026-11-25 03:58:13 status installed libc-bin:amd64 2.39-0ubuntu8
+```
+
+(Log paths vary by distribution; on systemd systems much of this lives in the journal instead, see module 07.)
+
+## Identify before you display
+Use `file` before assuming something is text:
+
+```bash
+file unknown.bin
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+unknown.bin: ELF 64-bit LSB pie executable, x86-64, ...
+```
+
+Printing arbitrary binary data with `cat` floods your terminal with control characters: the display can garble, beep, or become unreadable until you run `reset`. Worse, a maliciously crafted file can exploit that trust. The habit: unknown file, `file` first; if it is binary, inspect it with purpose-built tools (`stat`, `strings` for readable fragments) rather than dumping it.
+

+file backup
+stat backup
+head -c 200 backup | less
+```
+
+`file` reports the real type (an archive, a database, an image). `head -c 200` reads only 200 bytes, piped into `less` so even those cannot flood the display. Never `cat` a file this size.
+</details>

+tail -f application.log        # watch new lines live while they reproduce the issue
+```
+
+Add a search in a pager (`less application.log`, then `/error`) when "somewhere" is bigger than the tail.
+</details>

+**Q1.** Why is `cat huge.log` worse than `less huge.log`, beyond convenience?

+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

+`cat` streams the whole file through the terminal, which must render megabytes of output while you wait, and the part you wanted scrolls away. `less` reads on demand, starts instantly, and lets you search.

+</details>
