 ## Common mistakes
-- Using `cat` on huge files.
-- Treating binary output as meaningful text.
-- Forgetting to stop `tail -f`.
+- Using `cat` on huge files and waiting while the terminal drowns.
+- Treating binary output as meaningful text (or letting it garble the terminal; `reset` fixes the display).
+- Forgetting `tail -f` is still running in a background tab.
+- Overwriting a file with `>` when `>>` (append) was intended.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Choose the reader based on file size and purpose. Identify unknown files before displaying them.
+Match the reader to the file: `cat` for short, `less` for long, `head`/`tail` for ends, `tail -f` for live logs. Identify unknown files with `file` before displaying anything.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Distinguish root, regular, and system accounts.
-- Read UID, GID, and group membership information.
-- Locate account configuration files safely.
+- Distinguish root, regular, and system accounts, and read UID/GID identity information.
+- Locate and safely read the account configuration files.
+- Create and modify test accounts in a disposable environment without breaking anything real.
 
 ## Core concepts
-Each process runs with user and group identity information. A **UID** identifies a user; a **GID** identifies a group. Root normally has UID 0 and broad administrative authority. System accounts run services and usually should not be used for interactive work. Regular accounts are used by people.
+Every process on Linux runs with an identity: a **UID** (user ID) and one or more **GIDs** (group IDs). The kernel checks these numbers, not names, when deciding what a process may do. Three kinds of accounts matter:
 
-Important files:
-- `/etc/passwd`: account metadata, including username, UID, primary GID, home directory, and login shell. Password hashes are not normally stored here.
-- `/etc/shadow`: password hash and password-aging data; restricted access.
-- `/etc/group`: group names, GIDs, and supplementary member lists.
-- `/etc/sudoers` and `/etc/sudoers.d/`: sudo policy. Edit with `visudo`, which validates syntax.
+- **root (UID 0):** the superuser. Most permission checks simply do not apply to it. Reserved for administration.
+- **System accounts (low UIDs, often 1-999):** identities that run services, like `www-data` or `postgres`. They own files and processes so a compromised service does not become root. They normally have no valid password and a non-login shell, and should never be used interactively.
+- **Regular accounts (UID 1000+ on most distributions):** people. Daily work belongs here.
+
+Groups collect users for shared access: a file can grant its group read or write access, and every member inherits it. Each user has one **primary** group and any number of **supplementary** groups.
 
-Do not publish `/etc/shadow` contents or copy password hashes into notes.
+### The account files
+- `/etc/passwd`: account metadata, one line per account: username, placeholder `x`, UID, primary GID, description, home directory, login shell. World-readable; password hashes are **not** here (they moved out decades ago precisely because this file must be readable).
+- `/etc/shadow`: password hashes and password-aging data. Readable only by root (and the `shadow` group). Protect it accordingly: never paste its contents into tickets, notes, or chat.
+- `/etc/group`: group names, GIDs, and supplementary member lists.
+- `/etc/sudoers` and `/etc/sudoers.d/`: who may use `sudo` and how. **Always edit with `visudo`**, which checks syntax before saving; a broken sudoers file can lock every administrator out of privilege.
 
 ## Commands
 ```bash
@@ -25,35 +29,132 @@ groups
 getent passwd "$USER"
 getent group sudo
 ```
-`getent` queries configured account databases, which may include network identity sources, not just local files.
 
-Administrative account operations (distribution options differ):
+Sample output:
+
+```text
+$ id
+uid=1000(learner) gid=1000(learner) groups=1000(learner),27(sudo)
+$ getent passwd learner
+learner:x:1000:1000:Learner:/home/learner:/bin/bash
+```
+
+`getent` queries the configured account databases, which may include network identity sources (LDAP, AD), not just the local files, so it is the most truthful way to ask "what accounts exist here?"
+
+### Administrative operations (disposable VM only)
+```bash
+sudo useradd -m -s /bin/bash labuser
+sudo passwd labuser
+sudo groupadd labteam
+sudo usermod -aG sudo labuser      # Debian/Ubuntu: grant admin
+sudo usermod -aG labteam labuser   # add to a team group
+id labuser
+```
+
+- `useradd -m` creates the home directory; `-s` sets the login shell (the default may be a non-interactive one).
+- On Red Hat-family systems the administrative group is `wheel`, not `sudo`.
+- `-aG` **appends** supplementary groups. `usermod -G` *without* `-a` replaces the entire supplementary list, a classic way to accidentally strip someone's `sudo` membership. Verify with `id` after every change.
+- Group membership applies at login: a user already logged in must start a new session to see new groups.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Account creation belongs in a disposable VM. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Read your own identity: run `whoami`, `id`, and `getent passwd "$USER"`, and identify your UID, primary group, and every supplementary group by name.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+whoami
+id
+getent passwd "$USER"
+```
+
+In `id` output like `uid=1000(learner) gid=1000(learner) groups=1000(learner),27(sudo)`, your UID is 1000, primary group `learner`, and `sudo` is a supplementary group, meaning this account may use `sudo`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** In a disposable VM: create user `labuser` with a home directory and Bash shell, set a password, create group `labteam`, add `labuser` to it, and prove the membership.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
 ```bash
 sudo useradd -m -s /bin/bash labuser
 sudo passwd labuser
-sudo usermod -aG sudo labuser   # Debian/Ubuntu convention
 sudo groupadd labteam
 sudo usermod -aG labteam labuser
+id labuser
+```
+
+The last command shows `groups=...(labuser),...(labteam)`. If it does not, re-check that you typed `-aG`, not `-G`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Find the accounts on your system that can actually log in interactively: list entries in `/etc/passwd` whose shell is a real shell.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+grep -E '/(bash|sh|zsh)$' /etc/passwd
+```
+
+Typical result: `root` and the regular user accounts. System accounts point at `/usr/sbin/nologin` or `/bin/false`, which is how a service account is prevented from logging in even if its password were known.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** Clean up the test account from exercise 2, safely.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+id labuser                 # confirm this is the disposable account
+sudo userdel -r labuser    # removes the account and its home directory
+sudo groupdel labteam
+id labuser                 # confirms: no such user
 ```
-On some Red Hat-family systems, the administrative group is `wheel` rather than `sudo`. `-aG` appends supplementary groups; omitting `-a` can replace the existing supplementary group list. Verify with `id labuser`.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Inspect your own `id`, `groups`, and `getent passwd "$USER"` output.
-- In a disposable VM, create a test account and group, add the account to the group, and verify membership.
-- Remove the test account only after confirming it is the account you created.
-- Hint: `userdel -r labuser` removes the account and its home directory; use only for the disposable account.
+Only ever run `userdel -r` on an account you created for the lab, after confirming the name.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Does `/etc/passwd` contain password hashes? Where are they, and why the split?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. Hashes live in `/etc/shadow`, readable only by root. `/etc/passwd` must stay world-readable because every program resolving names to UIDs reads it; keeping hashes out of it keeps them away from every user on the system.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What is wrong with `sudo usermod -G labteam labuser` when the intent was to *add* a group?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Without `-a`, `-G` replaces the user's entire supplementary group list with just `labteam`, silently removing memberships like `sudo`. The correct command is `sudo usermod -aG labteam labuser`.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Why do services run as dedicated system accounts instead of root?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Containment. If the service is compromised, the attacker inherits only that account's limited permissions instead of the whole machine.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Assuming `/etc/passwd` contains password hashes.
-- Editing `/etc/sudoers` with a normal text editor rather than `visudo`.
-- Using `usermod -G` without `-a` and unintentionally removing group memberships.
-- Giving service accounts interactive shells without a reason.
+- Editing `/etc/sudoers` with a plain editor instead of `visudo`.
+- Using `usermod -G` without `-a` and destroying group memberships.
+- Giving service accounts interactive shells "temporarily" and forgetting.
+- Expecting new group membership to apply to sessions that are already logged in.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-UID/GID identity drives ownership and access. Protect shadow data and grant administrative rights deliberately.
+UID/GID numbers drive every ownership and access decision. Know the three account kinds, read identity with `id` and `getent`, guard `/etc/shadow`, and append groups with `-aG` every single time.
 
 ## Further practice
-Read `man passwd`, `man shadow`, `man useradd`, and `man usermod`.
+Read `man passwd`, `man shadow`, `man useradd`, and `man usermod`, then list the system accounts on your own machine and note what each one runs.
 
 ## References
 - [passwd(5)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/passwd.5.html)

 ## Learning objectives
-- Interpret read, write, and execute permissions.
-- Use symbolic and octal modes.
+- Interpret read, write, and execute permissions on files *and* directories, which mean different things.
+- Use symbolic and octal modes fluently, in both directions.
 - Change ownership and use sudo responsibly.
 
-## Permission model
-For ordinary files:
-- `r` (4): read contents.
-- `w` (2): modify contents.
-- `x` (1): execute the file, subject to other requirements.
+## The permission model
+Every file and directory carries three permission sets, for three classes of identity:
 
-For directories:
-- `r`: list names.
-- `w`: create, remove, or rename entries (normally combined with execute).
-- `x`: traverse/search the directory.
+- **user/owner (`u`):** the account that owns the file.
+- **group (`g`):** accounts in the file's group.
+- **other (`o`):** everyone else.
 
-The three classes are user/owner (`u`), group (`g`), and other (`o`). Example `-rwxr-xr--` means owner has `rwx`, group `r-x`, others `r--`.
+Each set has three bits, but their meaning depends on whether the object is a file or a directory:
+
+| Bit | On a file | On a directory |
+|---|---|---|
+| `r` (4) | read the contents | list the names inside |
+| `w` (2) | modify the contents | create, delete, rename entries (with `x`) |
+| `x` (1) | run it as a program | traverse: enter it and reach its entries |
+
+Read a real listing:
+
+```text
+-rwxr-xr-- 1 learner analysts 4096 Nov 25 04:20 deploy.sh
+```
+
+The leading `-` says regular file. Then three triples: owner `rwx` (read, write, execute), group `r-x` (read and execute, no write), other `r--` (read only). Owner is `learner`, group is `analysts`.
+
+> [!IMPORTANT]
+> Directory `x` is the most misunderstood bit in Linux. It means *traversal*: without it you cannot open files inside the directory even if you can list their names with `r`, and even if the files themselves are readable. When "the file is readable but access is denied", check the execute bit on every directory in the path.
 
 ### Octal modes
+Each triple adds up: read 4, write 2, execute 1.
+
 | Mode | Owner | Group | Other | Typical use |
 |---|---|---|---|---|
-| 755 | rwx | r-x | r-x | Publicly traversable executable directory |
-| 644 | rw- | r-- | r-- | Ordinary readable file |
-| 700 | rwx | --- | --- | Private directory |
-| 600 | rw- | --- | --- | Private file |
+| 755 | rwx | r-x | r-x | Programs and public directories |
+| 644 | rw- | r-- | r-- | Ordinary readable files |
+| 700 | rwx | --- | --- | Private directories (`~/.ssh`) |
+| 600 | rw- | --- | --- | Private files (keys, secrets) |
+| 640 | rw- | r-- | --- | Files a team may read but not change |
 
-Octal digits add values: read 4, write 2, execute 1.
+To go the other direction (symbols to octal), read each triple as a three-bit number: `r-x` = 4+0+1 = 5.
 
 ## Commands
 ```bash
-chmod 640 report.txt
-chmod u+x script.sh
-chmod go-rwx private.txt
+chmod 640 report.txt        # octal: set exactly rw- r-- ---
+chmod u+x script.sh         # symbolic: add execute for the owner
+chmod go-rwx private.txt    # remove everything from group and other
+chmod -R u+rwX ~/shared/    # recursive; capital X sets x only on directories and already-executable files
 sudo chown alice:analysts report.txt
 sudo chgrp analysts report.txt
 ```
-`chmod` changes mode bits. `chown` changes owner and optionally group; `chgrp` changes group. Ownership changes usually require root or suitable capabilities.
 
-`sudo command` runs a command under an authorized policy, commonly as root. `su - username` starts a login shell as another user and normally requires that account's password; `sudo -iu username` may be used where policy permits.
+`chmod` changes mode bits; symbolic form adjusts relative to the current mode (`+`, `-`, `=`), octal form replaces it entirely. `chown` changes owner and optionally group; `chgrp` changes only the group. Ownership changes almost always require root.
+
+### sudo: borrowed privilege
+`sudo command` runs one command with elevated privilege, as authorized by the sudoers policy (module 06 lesson 1), and logs the attempt. `sudo -iu username` starts a login shell as another user where policy permits; `su - username` does the same using *that account's* password.
+
+Two disciplines:
+
+- Run **one** privileged command, not a privileged session, whenever one command will do.
+- `sudo` writes as root. Careless use (`sudo` on editors, package installs into home, redirected output with `>`) leaves **root-owned files inside a user's home directory**, which later breaks that user's own programs.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+

+mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/private
+touch ~/linux-lab/private/secret.txt
+chmod 600 ~/linux-lab/private/secret.txt
+chmod 700 ~/linux-lab/private
+ls -ld ~/linux-lab/private ~/linux-lab/private/secret.txt
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+drwx------ 2 learner learner 4096 Nov 25 04:22 /home/learner/linux-lab/private
+-rw------- 1 learner learner    0 Nov 25 04:22 /home/learner/linux-lab/private/secret.txt
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Write the octal mode for each requirement, then apply one of them: (a) owner read+write, group read, other nothing; (b) everyone reads and executes, only owner writes; (c) owner everything, nobody else anything.

+chmod 640 ~/linux-lab/team.txt     # group-readable, not world-readable
+ls -l ~/linux-lab/team.txt

+```
+
+The `640` mode plus shared group membership is the standard pattern for team-readable files. Remove the test account when done (`sudo userdel -r labuser`).
+</details>

+**Exercise 4.** A script at `~/linux-lab/run.sh` will not execute: you get "Permission denied" even though you own it. Diagnose and fix it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls -l ~/linux-lab/run.sh
+```
+
+The file almost certainly lacks the execute bit (`-rw-rw-r--`). Ownership alone does not make a file executable:
+
+```bash
+chmod u+x ~/linux-lab/run.sh
+~/linux-lab/run.sh
+```
+</details>

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** A directory has mode `666` (everyone reads and writes). Can anyone `cd` into it or open its files?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. Without the execute/traverse bit on the directory, nobody can enter it or reach its contents, whatever the read and write bits say. This is why directories are normally `755`, `750`, or `700`.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Why is `chmod 777` almost never the right fix for an access error?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It grants every account on the system full read, write, and execute access, replacing a solvable permission question (who *should* reach this?) with "everyone can do anything". The right fix names the correct owner, group, and minimal mode.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Convert `-rwxr-x---` to octal, and `654` to symbols.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

-- Set the file to `600`, directory to `700`, and inspect with `ls -l` and `stat`.
-- In a disposable VM, change ownership to a test user and verify with `stat`.
-- Hint: use `chmod 755` only when group and other users genuinely need directory traversal or execution.
+`rwx`=7, `r-x`=5, `---`=0, so `750`. And `654` is `rw-`, `r-x`, `r--`, written `-rw-r-xr--`.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Thinking execute on a directory means running it; it means traversal/search.
-- Setting `777` to “fix” access errors. This usually grants far more access than needed.
+- Thinking execute on a directory means "run it"; it means traversal.
+- Reaching for `777` to silence an access error.
 - Forgetting directory execute permission when troubleshooting file access.
-- Using `sudo` for every command, which can create root-owned files in a user's home directory.
+- Using `sudo` for everything and littering home directories with root-owned files.
+- Changing ownership recursively with `chown -R` before checking what the tree contains.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Permissions are an access-control boundary. Apply the narrowest mode that supports the task.
+Permissions are an access-control boundary with three classes and three bits whose meaning flips between files and directories. Apply the narrowest mode that supports the task, and let sudo elevate one command at a time.

-Read `man chmod`, `man chown`, `man sudo`, and `man su`.
+Read `man chmod`, `man chown`, and `man sudo`, then audit your own home directory: `ls -la ~` and explain every mode you see.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Recognize SUID, SGID, and sticky directory permissions.
-- Inspect special permission bits.
-- Explain least privilege and defensive privilege auditing.
+- Recognize SUID, SGID, and sticky directory permissions in listings.
+- Inspect special permission bits on a real system.
+- Explain least privilege and perform a defensive privilege audit without breaking anything.
 
-## Special bits
-- **SUID (set-user-ID):** on an executable file, a process may run with the file owner's effective UID. It is commonly represented by `s` in the owner's execute position.
-- **SGID (set-group-ID):** on an executable, may set the effective group ID; on a directory, newly created entries commonly inherit the directory's group.
-- **Sticky bit:** on a shared writable directory, users generally may remove or rename only entries they own (or where they have suitable privilege). `/tmp` commonly uses this bit.
+## The three special bits
+Beyond `rwx`, three extra bits change how execution and shared directories behave:
 
-Octal special-bit prefixes: SUID `4`, SGID `2`, sticky `1`. For example, `chmod 4755 file` sets SUID plus mode `755`; do not apply special bits casually.
+- **SUID (set-user-ID, value 4):** on an executable file, the process runs with the **file owner's** effective UID instead of the caller's. Shown as `s` in the owner's execute position. This is how `passwd` lets an ordinary user change a password stored in root-owned `/etc/shadow`: `/usr/bin/passwd` is SUID root, so it briefly acts with root's authority for that one, carefully reviewed task.
+- **SGID (set-group-ID, value 2):** on an executable, the process runs with the file's group. On a **directory**, new files and subdirectories inherit the directory's group instead of the creator's primary group, which is exactly what shared team directories need. Shown as `s` in the group's execute position.
+- **Sticky bit (value 1):** on a shared writable directory, users may delete or rename only entries they own. `/tmp` is the classic example: everyone may create files there, but you cannot remove someone else's. Shown as `t` in others' execute position.
 
-## Inspection
+Octal prefix: special bits form a leading digit, `chmod 4755 file` = SUID + `755`, `chmod 2770 dir` = SGID + `770`, `chmod 1777 dir` = sticky + `777`.
+
+## Inspecting them
 ```bash
 ls -l /usr/bin/passwd
+ls -ld /tmp
 find /usr/bin -type f -perm -4000 -print 2>/dev/null
 find / -xdev -type f -perm -2000 -print 2>/dev/null
-ls -ld /tmp
 ```
-`-perm -4000` finds files with the SUID bit set; `-xdev` avoids crossing filesystem boundaries. Results vary by distribution and installed software. A SUID file is not automatically vulnerable; it deserves review of ownership, package origin, expected behavior, and updates.
+
+Sample output:
+
+```text
+-rwsr-xr-x 1 root root 68208 Nov 12  2024 /usr/bin/passwd
+drwxrwxrwt 14 root root 4096 Nov 25 04:25 /tmp
+```
+
+`-perm -4000` finds files with SUID set; `-perm -2000` finds SGID; `-xdev` keeps the search on one filesystem. On any normal system you will find a handful of SUID binaries (`passwd`, `sudo`, `su`, `ping` on some systems). A SUID file is **not automatically a vulnerability**, but each one is code that runs with elevated authority, so each deserves review: expected package origin, correct owner, current updates.
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> Never experiment with special bits on a production host, and never set SUID on scripts. Script SUID behavior is platform-dependent and historically insecure; if a task needs privilege, use a reviewed SUID binary or a narrow sudo rule.
 
 ## Least privilege
-Give a user, service, or process only the permissions required for its task, for only as long as needed. Avoid broad sudo rules, world-writable configuration, unnecessary SUID programs, and shared accounts. Review changes and remove temporary privileges.
+The organizing principle for everything in this module: **give a user, service, or process only the permissions its task requires, for only as long as the task lasts.** In practice that means:
+
+- No broad `sudo` rules ("this user may run anything") when three specific commands would do.
+- No world-writable configuration or shared directories without the sticky bit.
+- No unnecessary SUID programs; audit the ones you have.
+- No shared human accounts; shared access belongs to groups.
+- Temporary privileges get reviewed and removed.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal (a VM for the write operations). Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Explain the permissions on `/tmp` from its `ls -ld` output, field by field, including the final `t`.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls -ld /tmp
+```
+
+`drwxrwxrwt`: a directory; owner root has `rwx`; group root has `rwx`; others have `rwx` **plus sticky** (`t`). The world-writable mode lets every user create temp files; the sticky bit stops users deleting each other's files. World-writable *without* sticky would be a problem; with sticky it is the intended design.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** List the SUID binaries on your system, pick one, and identify which package owns it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+find / -xdev -type f -perm -4000 -print 2>/dev/null
+dpkg -S /usr/bin/passwd      # Debian/Ubuntu
+rpm -qf /usr/bin/passwd      # Fedora/RHEL
+```
+
+Every result should trace back to a package from your distribution. A SUID binary no package claims is a finding worth investigating.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Build a proper shared directory: create `/srv/team` owned by group `labteam`, group-writable, with SGID so new files stay in `labteam`, then create a file inside and verify its group.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+sudo groupadd labteam
+sudo usermod -aG labteam "$USER"
+sudo mkdir -p /srv/team
+sudo chown root:labteam /srv/team
+sudo chmod 2770 /srv/team
+# new login session (or: newgrp labteam), then:
+touch /srv/team/shared.txt
+ls -l /srv/team/shared.txt
+```
+
+The new file's group is `labteam` even though your primary group is your own; that inheritance is the SGID bit working. Mode `2770` = SGID + owner/group full access + nothing for others.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** In `-rwsr-xr-x`, what does the `s` mean, and whose privileges does the program run with?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+SUID is set: the program runs with the file *owner's* effective UID (here root), no matter who launches it.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Two teammates share `/srv/team` and keep creating files the other cannot edit, because new files land in each creator's private group. Which bit fixes the directory, and why?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+SGID on the directory: new entries inherit the directory's group (`labteam`), so group write permission actually reaches both teammates.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** You find a SUID-root binary in `/home/someuser/bin` that no package owns. React?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Inspect `/tmp` permissions and explain the sticky bit.
-- List SUID files on your own VM and identify which package owns one using the package manager.
-- Do not modify or execute unknown privileged binaries.
-- Hint: use `stat` and your distribution's package-query tool.
+Treat it as a security finding, not a curiosity: do not execute it, record it (`stat`, `file`, hash), and report/remove it through your change process. Unowned SUID-root files in user-writable locations are a classic persistence and privilege-escalation technique.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Treating every SUID binary as an exploitable vulnerability.
-- Removing special bits from system files without understanding package expectations.
-- Setting SUID on scripts; behavior is platform- and kernel-dependent and is generally not a safe design.
+- Treating every SUID binary as an exploitable vulnerability; review, don't panic.
+- Removing special bits from system files without understanding what the package expects.
+- Setting SUID on scripts.
+- Making shared directories `777` instead of `2770` with a proper group.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Special bits affect execution identity and shared-directory behavior. Audit them carefully; do not alter them as an experiment on a production host.
+SUID borrows the owner's identity, SGID shares a group, sticky protects entries in shared directories. Audit special bits regularly, and let least privilege decide every grant.

-Study Linux capabilities and service account design at a conceptual level after completing this course.
+After this course, study Linux capabilities (`man 7 capabilities`) and how services drop privileges; they are the modern refinement of everything in this lesson.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain PID, PPID, process states, and job control.
-- Inspect and monitor processes.
-- Send signals and distinguish graceful termination from forced termination.
+- Explain PID, PPID, process states, and shell job control.
+- Inspect and monitor processes with the right tool for each question.
+- Send signals correctly: graceful termination first, forced termination last.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A **process** is a running program instance. It has a process ID (PID), a parent process ID (PPID), credentials, open file descriptors, and memory mappings. A process can be foreground or background relative to a shell. Common states include running, sleeping, stopped, and zombie.
+A **process** is a running instance of a program. Each has a **PID** (process ID), a **PPID** (parent's PID), credentials (the UID/GIDs it runs with), open files, and memory. Processes form a tree: every process was started by a parent, and PID 1 (the init system, usually `systemd`) is the ancestor of everything.
+
+Common states you will see in listings: **R** running, **S** sleeping (waiting for something, the normal idle state), **T** stopped (suspended), **Z** zombie (finished, waiting for its parent to collect its exit status).
 
 ## Commands
 ```bash
-ps
-ps aux
-ps -ef
-pgrep -a ssh
-pidof systemd
-top
-htop
-uptime
-free -h
+ps            # processes of this terminal
+ps aux        # everything, BSD style
+ps -ef        # everything, with PPIDs
+pgrep -a ssh  # find by name, show the command line
+top           # live view; q to quit
+htop          # friendlier live view (may need installing)
+uptime        # load averages
+free -h       # memory
 ```
-`ps` snapshots process information; `top` refreshes interactively; `htop` is an optional enhanced monitor. `pgrep -a` finds matching process names and prints command lines. `pidof` may not be installed or useful for every program.
 
-### Job control
+Which one answers which question:
+
+- "What's running?" → `ps aux` (or `ps -ef` when you need parent IDs).
+- "What's eating CPU right now?" → `top` / `htop`, sorted by CPU.
+- "Is the service up?" → `pgrep -a name`.
+- "Is the box overloaded?" → `uptime` first: the three load averages over 1, 5, and 15 minutes, compared against your CPU count.
+
+## Job control: processes and your shell
 ```bash
 sleep 300
 # press Ctrl-Z to suspend
 jobs
-bg
-fg
+bg        # resume in background
+fg        # bring back to foreground
 ```
-Append `&` to start a command in the background: `sleep 300 &`. `jobs` reports jobs belonging to the current shell; it is not a system-wide process listing.
 
-### Signals and termination
+- Append `&` to start in the background: `sleep 300 &`.
+- `Ctrl-Z` suspends the foreground process; `bg` resumes it in the background; `fg` returns it to the foreground.
+- `jobs` lists **this shell's** jobs (`%1`, `%2`, ...). A job number is not a PID and `jobs` is not a system-wide view; it only knows what this shell started.
+
+## Signals and termination
+A signal is a small message delivered to a process. The two you will use constantly:
+
+- **SIGTERM (15):** "please shut down." The process may save state, close connections, and exit cleanly. It may also catch and ignore the request. Always the first choice.
+- **SIGKILL (9):** the kernel destroys the process immediately. It cannot be caught, blocked, or ignored, and nothing gets cleaned up: temporary files, locks, and half-written data remain. Last resort only.
+
 ```bash
 kill -TERM 1234
-kill -KILL 1234
-kill -l
-killall -TERM sleep
-nohup sleep 300 >/tmp/sleep.log 2>&1 &
+kill -KILL 1234     # only after TERM failed
+kill -l             # list all signals
+killall -TERM sleep # by name: check what matches first with pgrep -a
+```
+
+Replace `1234` with a PID you own and have verified with `ps` or `pgrep`. Signaling a guessed PID is how the wrong service dies. `killall` selects by name and may match more processes than you intended.
+
+To survive logout, `nohup command &` keeps a command running after the terminal hangs up (output goes to `nohup.out` unless redirected). It is a convenience, not a service manager; anything that must run reliably belongs to systemd (next lesson).
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Start `sleep 300` in the background, find its PID two different ways, then terminate it gracefully and prove it is gone.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+sleep 300 &
+jobs -l
+pgrep -a sleep
+kill -TERM <PID>
+pgrep -a sleep || echo "gone"
+```
+
+`jobs -l` shows the shell's jobs with PIDs; `pgrep -a sleep` finds it system-wide by name. After SIGTERM, `pgrep` finds nothing.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Suspend a foreground process, move it to the background, then bring it back and stop it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+sleep 600
+# Ctrl-Z
+jobs
+bg %1
+jobs
+fg %1
+# Ctrl-C
 ```
-`SIGTERM` (15) requests graceful termination; the process may clean up or ignore it. `SIGKILL` (9) cannot be caught and should be a last resort. Replace `1234` with a PID you own and have verified. `killall name` targets processes by name and can affect more than intended.
 
-`nohup command &` can keep a command running after a hangup, commonly writing output to `nohup.out` when output is not redirected. It is not a service manager. `killall name` sends a signal to processes selected by name; verify the exact target and local implementation before using it because multiple processes may match.
+`%1` is the job number, valid only in this shell. `Ctrl-C` sends SIGINT, the interactive interrupt.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Find the five processes currently using the most memory on your system, without opening an interactive tool.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -n 6
+```
+
+`--sort=-%mem` sorts by memory descending (the leading `-` reverses); `head -n 6` keeps the header plus five rows.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** A runaway process ignores `kill -TERM`. Write the escalation sequence you would actually type, in order, starting from not knowing its PID.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+pgrep -a runaway          # confirm PID and that it's really the right process
+kill -TERM <PID>          # graceful request
+sleep 3; pgrep -a runaway # still there?
+kill -KILL <PID>          # last resort: kernel removes it
+```
+
+The pause and re-check between steps is what keeps this safe and deliberate.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why is `kill -9` a bad *first* response to a stuck process?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+SIGKILL gives the process no chance to clean up: open files, locks, database transactions, and temporary state are abandoned mid-write. Try SIGTERM, confirm, and only escalate when cleanup has demonstrably failed.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** `jobs` shows nothing, but you can see your process in `ps aux`. Explain.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`jobs` only tracks children of the current shell. If the process was started from another terminal, detached, or reparented, this shell has no job entry for it; the system-wide `ps` still lists it.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** What is a zombie process, and whose job is it to clear it?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

-- Start `sleep 120 &`, find its PID with `jobs -l` or `pgrep`.
-- Stop it with `kill -TERM PID`.
-- Run `top` and identify CPU and memory columns.
-- Hint: verify the PID before sending a signal.
+A finished process whose parent has not yet collected its exit status; it holds a slot in the process table but no memory or CPU. The parent clears it by reading the status; if the parent is broken and dies, PID 1 adopts and reaps the zombie.
+</details>

 - Assuming `jobs` shows every process on the machine.

 ## Key takeaways
-Use process inspection before signaling. Prefer graceful termination and use a service manager for managed services.
+Inspect before you signal: `ps`, `pgrep`, `top`. Terminate gracefully first, forcibly last. Shell jobs and system processes are two different views of the same activity.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain services and daemons.
-- Inspect and manage systemd units.
-- Read service logs with journalctl.
+- Explain what a service/daemon is and how a service manager supervises one.
+- Inspect and manage systemd units without confusion between similar commands.
+- Read service logs with `journalctl`, filtered to exactly what you need.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A **daemon** is a background process that provides a service. **systemd** is a common init and service manager, but not universal. A **unit** describes a managed resource such as a service, socket, timer, or mount.
+A **daemon** is a background process that provides a service: `sshd` answers remote logins, `cron` runs scheduled jobs, a web server answers requests. Daemons are not meant to be started and babysat by hand; that is the job of the **init system / service manager**.
 
-Stopping a service asks the service manager to stop the managed unit and may trigger configured cleanup. Killing a process sends a signal to one process and can bypass service-level management. A service manager may restart a killed process according to policy.
+**systemd** is the service manager on most mainstream distributions (Ubuntu, Debian, Fedora, RHEL, Arch), but it is not universal: containers, WSL sessions, and some minimal systems run without it. Check before assuming.
+
+A **unit** is systemd's description of a managed resource. The common types: `.service` (a daemon), `.timer` (scheduled activation, the modern cron), `.socket` (start on connection), `.mount`. You will mostly touch services and timers.
+
+### Stop a service vs kill a process
+Stopping a service (`systemctl stop`) asks the manager to shut the unit down through its configured procedure. Killing a process sends a signal to one PID and bypasses that management entirely, and the manager may simply restart the process according to its policy. For managed services, always talk to the manager.
 
 ## Commands (systemd systems)
 ```bash
 systemctl status ssh
 systemctl is-active ssh
 systemctl list-units --type=service --state=running
+systemctl list-unit-files --type=service
 sudo systemctl start ssh
 sudo systemctl stop ssh
 sudo systemctl restart ssh
+sudo systemctl reload ssh
 sudo systemctl enable ssh
 sudo systemctl disable ssh
+```
+
+The unit may be named `ssh` (Debian/Ubuntu) or `sshd` (RPM family); check with `list-unit-files` when in doubt.
+
+The two pairs everyone confuses:
+
+- `start`/`stop` control what is happening **now**.
+- `enable`/`disable` control what happens **at boot** (by creating or removing a symlink). Enabling does not start; starting does not enable. `sudo systemctl enable --now ssh` does both.
+
+`reload` asks a service to re-read its configuration without dropping connections, when the service supports it; `restart` is the heavier hammer.
+
+Sample status output:
+
+```text
+$ systemctl status ssh
+● ssh.service - OpenBSD Secure Shell server
+     Loaded: loaded (...; enabled; preset: enabled)
+     Active: active (running) since Tue 2026-11-25 03:58:11 EET; 20min ago
+   Main PID: 812 (sshd)
+```
+
+The leading `●` is green when active. `Loaded: ... enabled` means it starts at boot; `Active: active (running)` means it is up now.
+
+## Reading the journal
+```bash
 journalctl -u ssh --since today
-journalctl -b -p warning
+journalctl -u ssh -f                    # follow live
+journalctl -b -p warning                # this boot, warnings and worse
+journalctl --since "2026-11-25 03:00" --until "2026-11-25 04:00"
+journalctl -b -1                        # the previous boot (crashes)
 ```
-The service may be named `ssh` (Debian/Ubuntu) or `sshd` (many RPM-based systems). `enable` configures startup at boot; it does not necessarily start the service immediately. `start` starts it now; it does not necessarily enable it at boot.
 
-`journalctl -u UNIT` filters by unit. `-b` selects the current boot; `-p warning` filters priority. Access to some logs may require elevated permissions or membership in a log-reading group.
+- `-u UNIT` filters to one service; `-f` follows like `tail -f`.
+- `-b` selects a boot: current, or `-b -1` for the previous one, invaluable after an unexpected reboot.
+- `-p warning` filters by priority (debug < info < notice < warning < err < crit < alert < emerg).
+- Full journal access may require root or membership in the `systemd-journal` group.
+
+> [!CAUTION]
+> Never stop or restart `ssh`/`sshd` (or networking) on a remote machine unless you have an out-of-band console recovery path. Locking yourself out is one command away.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal; use a disposable VM for start/stop practice. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Pick a running service, read its full status, and answer from the output: is it enabled at boot, since when is it active, and what is its main PID?

+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>

+systemctl is-active cron        # inactive
+sudo systemctl start cron

+</details>

+```

+</details>

diff --git a/platforms/linux/linux-essentials/08-text-processing-and-pipelines/01-streams-redirection-and-pipes.md b/platforms/linux/linux-essentials/08-text-processing-and-pipelines/01-streams-redirection-and-pipes.md

--- a/platforms/linux/linux-essentials/08-text-processing-and-pipelines/01-streams-redirection-and-pipes.md
+++ b/platforms/linux/linux-essentials/08-text-processing-and-pipelines/01-streams-redirection-and-pipes.md

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain stdin, stdout, stderr, and file descriptors.
-- Redirect and append output.
-- Build pipelines and interpret exit codes.
+- Explain stdin, stdout, stderr, and their file descriptor numbers.
+- Redirect and append output, and combine or split streams deliberately.
+- Build pipelines and interpret exit codes, including in chains of commands.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A process normally starts with three standard streams:
-- stdin (file descriptor 0): input.
-- stdout (1): normal output.
-- stderr (2): diagnostic output.
+Every process starts with three standard streams:
 
-A **pipe** connects one command's stdout to the next command's stdin. Redirection connects streams to files or other destinations.
+- **stdin** (file descriptor 0): where it reads input.
+- **stdout** (file descriptor 1): where it writes normal output.
+- **stderr** (file descriptor 2): where it writes diagnostics and errors.
+
+The separation is deliberate and useful: a program's *results* flow on stdout while its *complaints* flow on stderr, so you can capture, filter, or discard each independently. A **pipe** (`|`) connects one command's stdout to the next command's stdin. **Redirection** connects a stream to a file instead.
+
+This is the Unix philosophy in practice: small tools that each do one thing, glued together with streams into exactly the workflow you need.
 
 ## Syntax
 ```bash
-command > output.txt       # overwrite stdout
-command >> output.txt      # append stdout
-command < input.txt        # read stdin from file
-command 2> errors.txt      # redirect stderr
-command > all.txt 2>&1     # stdout and stderr to same destination
-command 2>&1 | less        # combine streams, then pipe
+command > output.txt       # stdout to file, overwriting
+command >> output.txt      # stdout to file, appending
+command < input.txt        # stdin from file
+command 2> errors.txt      # stderr to file
+command > all.txt 2>&1     # stdout AND stderr to the same file
+command 2>&1 | less        # combine streams, then pipe the mix
+command &> all.txt         # Bash shorthand for both streams
+```
+
+Order matters and it follows one rule: redirections are applied left to right. `> all.txt 2>&1` first points stdout at the file, then points stderr at wherever stdout now goes: both land in the file. Reversed, `2>&1 > all.txt` first points stderr at the *old* stdout (your terminal), then moves stdout to the file: errors still hit the screen.
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> `>` truncates the destination **before** the command runs, even if the command then fails. `cat important.txt > important.txt` destroys the file instantly. When you mean "add to the end", type `>>`.
+
+## Pipes and exit codes
+```bash
+printf 'alpha\nbeta\n' | grep beta
+printf 'c\nb\na\n' | sort | head -n 1
+```
+
+Each stage reads the previous stage's stdout. The pipeline's exit status in Bash is the status of its **last** command, which hides failures in the middle:
+
+```bash
+grep secret missing-file.txt | sort
+echo "status: $?"           # 0: sort succeeded even though grep failed
+set -o pipefail
+grep secret missing-file.txt | sort
+echo "status: $?"           # 2: the pipeline now reports grep's failure
+```
+
+`$?` always holds the most recent foreground command's status: `0` means success, anything else is a failure code. Scripts rely on `set -o pipefail` (module 12) so a broken stage cannot masquerade as success.
+
+### Command chaining with statuses
+- `a && b`: run `b` only if `a` **succeeded**. `mkdir out && cd out`.
+- `a || b`: run `b` only if `a` **failed**. `pgrep myapp || echo "not running"`.
+- `a ; b`: run both, regardless.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Run `ls /does-not-exist` and send its error message to `errors.txt` while letting any normal output reach the screen. Then check the exit status.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls /does-not-exist 2> errors.txt
+echo "$?"
+cat errors.txt
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+2
+ls: cannot access '/does-not-exist': No such file or directory
+```
+
+The error went to the file (descriptor 2), the status `2` is `ls`'s failure code.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Build a three-stage pipeline: list the names in `/usr/bin`, keep only those containing `sh`, and count them.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+ls /usr/bin | grep sh | wc -l
 ```
-Order matters: `> all.txt 2>&1` first redirects stdout, then points stderr to the same destination. `2>&1 > all.txt` has a different effect.
+
+`wc -l` counts lines. Each tool does one job; the pipe composes them. (You will learn `grep` properly in the next lesson.)
+</details>

+**Exercise 3.** Capture both stdout and stderr of `ls /tmp /does-not-exist` into one file, in the order the messages were produced.

 ```bash

+ls /tmp /does-not-exist > both.txt 2>&1

 ```
-The pipe passes text to `grep`. A pipeline's exit status is normally the status of its last command in Bash; `set -o pipefail` makes a pipeline fail if a command fails (with the rightmost nonzero status).
 
-### Command chaining
-- `a && b`: run `b` only if `a` succeeds.
-- `a || b`: run `b` only if `a` fails.
-- `a ; b`: run both sequentially regardless of the first status.
-- `$?`: status of the most recently completed foreground command (or pipeline).
+The `2>&1` must come **after** the file redirection so stderr follows stdout into the file.
+</details>

+```bash
+[ -d ~/linux-lab ] && mkdir -p ~/linux-lab/pipeline-out || echo missing
+```
+
+`[ -d ... ]` tests for the directory; `&&` runs the `mkdir` only when the test succeeds; `||` prints the message only when the chain so far failed.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** What is the difference between `>` and `>>`, and when does the difference become destructive?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`>` truncates the target file before the command runs; `>>` appends. It becomes destructive the moment you redirect into a file that already holds data you wanted to keep.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** `prog 2>&1 > out.txt` does not capture errors in `out.txt`. Why?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Left-to-right: `2>&1` first points stderr at the current stdout (the terminal), then `> out.txt` moves only stdout into the file. The correct order is `prog > out.txt 2>&1`.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** A pipeline's last command succeeded, so `$?` is 0. Does that prove the whole pipeline worked?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

+No. Without `pipefail`, `$?` reports only the last command. A middle stage can fail silently; `set -o pipefail` makes the pipeline report the first failure.

 ## Common mistakes
-- Using `>` when you meant to append; it truncates the destination.
-- Reversing redirection order.
-- Assuming a successful last pipeline command means every earlier command succeeded.
-- Forgetting that shell redirection is processed before the command runs.
+- Using `>` when `>>` was meant.
+- Reversing the order in `> file 2>&1`.
+- Trusting the last command's status for the whole pipeline.
+- Forgetting that the shell performs redirections before the command starts, so the file is truncated even when the command errors.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Streams and pipes let small tools work together. Redirect deliberately and check exit status.
+stdout carries results, stderr carries complaints, and pipes plus redirections route each where you want it. Quote the order as "file first, then stderr follows": `> file 2>&1`. Check `$?`, and use `pipefail` in anything scripted.

-## Core tools
+## grep: find the lines that matter
 ```bash
-grep -n -i 'failed' auth.log
-grep -E 'error|warning' application.log
-grep -Rni 'password' ./config
+grep -n -i 'failed' auth.log        # line numbers, case-insensitive
+grep -E 'error|warning' app.log     # extended regex: either word
+grep -Rni 'password' ./config       # recursive through a directory
+grep -c 'failed' auth.log           # count matching lines
+grep -v 'health-check' app.log      # invert: everything EXCEPT matches
 ```
-`grep` searches lines. `-n` prints line numbers, `-i` ignores case, `-E` enables extended regular expressions, and `-R` recursively searches directories. Avoid dumping secret files into shared logs or tickets.
 
+Key options: `-n` line numbers, `-i` ignore case, `-E` extended regular expressions, `-R` recursive, `-c` count, `-v` invert, `-o` print only the matched part.
+
+A beginner regex survival kit:
+
+- `^failed` matches lines *starting* with "failed"; `failed$` matches lines *ending* with it.
+- `.` matches any single character; `.*` matches any run of characters.
+- `[0-9]` matches a digit; `[0-9]{3}` matches exactly three.
+- `error|warning` (with `-E`) matches either alternative.
+
+> [!CAUTION]
+> When grepping for credentials or secrets during an investigation, results can contain real passwords or keys. Do not paste raw matches into shared tickets or chat.
+
+## sed: edit the stream
 ```bash
-sed 's/old/new/g' file.txt
-sed -n '1,5p' file.txt
+sed 's/old/new/g' file.txt          # replace every "old" with "new", print result
+sed -n '1,5p' file.txt              # print only lines 1-5
+sed '/^#/d' config.txt              # delete comment lines from the output
 ```
-`sed` is a stream editor. The first command replaces each `old` with `new` on each line in output; it does not modify the file in place. `-n` suppresses default printing, and `p` prints selected lines. In-place editing (`-i`) is platform-sensitive; make a backup first.
 
+`sed` is a stream editor: by default it **prints** the transformed text and never touches the original file. The substitution command `s/old/new/g` replaces per line (`g` = all occurrences on the line, not just the first). In-place editing (`sed -i`) rewrites the file; behavior differs between GNU and BSD sed, so take a backup first (`sed -i.bak ...` keeps the original as `file.bak`).
+
+## awk: work with columns
 ```bash
-awk '{print $1, $2}' file.txt
-awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/passwd
-awk '$3 > 1000 {print $1, $3}' /etc/passwd
+awk '{print $1, $2}' file.txt       # first two whitespace-separated fields
+awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/passwd    # colon-separated: usernames
+awk '$3 >= 1000 {print $1, $3}' /etc/passwd   # conditions on fields
 ```
-`awk` processes records (usually lines) and fields. `-F:` sets the field separator. `$1` is the first field; `$0` is the full record.
 
-Other useful commands:
+`awk` reads records (lines) split into fields: `$1` is the first field, `$0` the whole line, `-F:` sets the separator. A condition before the block filters which records the block applies to. This is the tool for "column questions" that `grep` cannot answer.
+
+## The supporting cast
 ```bash
-cut -d: -f1 /etc/passwd
-sort names.txt
-sort names.txt | uniq
-sort names.txt | uniq -c | sort -nr
-wc -l file.txt
-tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]' < file.txt
-command | tee output.txt
-printf '%s
-' file1 file2 | xargs -r ls -l --
+cut -d: -f1 /etc/passwd                      # simpler field extraction
+sort names.txt | uniq                        # unique values...
+sort names.txt | uniq -c | sort -nr          # ...or a ranked frequency table
+wc -l file.txt                               # count lines
+tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]' < file.txt        # normalize case
+command | tee output.txt                     # print AND save simultaneously
+printf '%s\0' f1 f2 | xargs -0 ls -l --      # build arguments safely
 ```
-`uniq` detects adjacent duplicate lines, so sort first for global counts. `xargs` builds command arguments from input; use `-0` with `find -print0` for filenames containing whitespace or unusual characters.
-
-## Progressive examples
-1. Case-insensitive failure lines:
-   `grep -ni 'failed' auth.log`
-2. Count failure lines:
-   `grep -ci 'failed' auth.log`
-3. Count source IP-like tokens in a controlled sample:
-   `grep -Eo '([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}' auth.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -nr`
-4. Extract colon-delimited usernames:
-   `cut -d: -f1 /etc/passwd`
-
-The IP regular expression above is illustrative token extraction, **not complete IPv4 validation**. It can match invalid octets such as 999 and does not validate context.
-
-## Sample log
-Save as `sample-auth.log`:
+
+Two traps to memorize: `uniq` only merges **adjacent** duplicates, so `sort` must come first; and filenames containing spaces break naive `xargs`, so use `find -print0 | xargs -0` for anything real.
+
+## Worked example: analyzing an authentication log
+Save this as `sample-auth.log` (addresses are documentation ranges, safe to use):
+
 ```text

 Nov 21 09:02:11 lab sshd[1204]: Accepted publickey for analyst from 192.0.2.25 port 50123 ssh2

 Nov 21 09:06:50 lab sshd[1221]: Failed password for invalid user test from 192.0.2.10 port 50155 ssh2
 ```

-## Hands-on practice: analyze the sample
-- Count failed-password lines.
-- Extract and rank IP-like tokens from failed lines.
-- Print only usernames from `/etc/passwd`.
-- Save matching lines to a report using `tee`.
-- Hint: filter first, then extract, then count with `sort | uniq -c`.
+Build the analysis in stages, checking each before adding the next:
+
+```bash
+grep 'Failed password' sample-auth.log | wc -l
+```
+
+```text
+4
+```
+
+```bash
+grep 'Failed password' sample-auth.log | grep -Eo '([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -nr
+```
+
+```text
+      3 192.0.2.10
+      1 198.51.100.7
+```
+
+Read the second pipeline left to right: keep failure lines, extract IP-shaped tokens, sort so duplicates touch, count them, rank descending. The pattern matches IP-*shaped* text, not valid IPv4 (it would accept `999.999.999.999`); it is extraction, not validation.
+

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why does `uniq -c` almost always follow `sort`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`uniq` merges only adjacent duplicate lines. Without sorting first, identical lines scattered through the input are counted separately.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Does `sed 's/root/admin/' /etc/passwd` change the file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+No. Without `-i`, sed prints the transformed stream to stdout and the file is untouched. (And do not run that with `-i` for real: editing account databases by hand is how systems break.)
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** In `awk -F: '{print $1}'`, what do `-F:` and `$1` mean?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`-F:` splits each line on colons, and `$1` is the first resulting field, the username in `/etc/passwd`.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Forgetting `uniq` only combines adjacent duplicates.
-- Using an overly broad regular expression and calling it validation.
-- Editing the original log while investigating.
-- Splitting filenames with spaces using unsafe `xargs` input.
+- Forgetting `uniq` needs sorted input.
+- Calling a loose regex "validation".
+- Editing the original log mid-investigation; work on a copy.
+- Letting `xargs` split on whitespace when filenames may contain spaces.
+- Reaching for a Python script when a four-stage pipeline answers the question in one line.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Text-processing commands are composable. Filter, extract, normalize, and count in separate pipeline stages.
+Filter (grep), transform (sed), extract (awk/cut), then count and rank (sort, uniq -c). Build pipelines one stage at a time and check each stage's output before adding the next.
 
 ## Further practice
-Use `awk` to count log events by field and compare the result with `grep -c`.
+Repeat the worked example with `awk` alone (hint: `awk '/Failed/' file | ...` versus a single `awk` program), then run the same analysis against a real log in `/var/log` on your VM.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain repositories and package dependencies.
-- Update indexes, install, remove, and inspect packages with APT.
-- Use dpkg for lower-level package inspection.
+- Explain repositories, dependencies, and package signatures.
+- Update indexes, install, remove, and inspect software with APT.
+- Use dpkg for the lower-level questions APT does not answer.
 
 ## Core concepts
-A package manager installs software from configured repositories, verifies package metadata/signatures according to repository configuration, resolves dependencies, tracks installed files, and supports upgrades and removal. Repositories are software sources maintained by a distribution or trusted provider.
+A package manager is the difference between installing software and *maintaining* it. It downloads packages from configured **repositories** (software sources maintained by the distribution or a trusted provider), verifies their signatures against the repository's keys, resolves **dependencies** (the other packages the software needs), tracks every installed file, and handles upgrades and removal cleanly. Installing software any other way (random downloads, `curl | sh`) abandons all of those guarantees.
 
-APT is the higher-level package manager on Debian and Ubuntu. `dpkg` works with local Debian package files and the installed package database.
+On Debian and Ubuntu, two tools share the work:
+
+- **APT** (`apt`) is the high-level manager: repositories, dependencies, upgrades.
+- **dpkg** is the low-level tool: the local package database and individual `.deb` files.
+
+## Commands
+```bash
+sudo apt update                 # refresh the repository indexes
+apt search openssh-client       # find packages by name/description
+apt show openssh-client         # metadata: version, size, dependencies
+sudo apt install tree           # install
+sudo apt upgrade                # upgrade installed packages
+sudo apt remove tree            # remove, keep configuration
+sudo apt purge tree             # remove including configuration
+apt list --installed            # everything installed
+dpkg -l openssh-client          # installed status of one package
+dpkg -S /usr/bin/ssh            # which package owns this file?
+dpkg -L openssh-client          # which files does this package own?
+```
+
+The pair everyone confuses: `apt update` refreshes the **list of available packages** and changes nothing on disk; `apt upgrade` actually installs newer versions. The routine is always `update` first, then `upgrade`.
+
+`remove` versus `purge` matters months later: `remove` leaves the package's configuration in `/etc` (so reinstalling restores your settings); `purge` deletes that too.
+
+### Local .deb files
+```bash
+sudo dpkg -i package.deb
+sudo apt --fix-broken install
+```
+
+`dpkg -i` installs a local file but does not fetch dependencies; if it complains about missing ones, `apt --fix-broken install` resolves them from the repositories. Only install `.deb` files from sources you trust as much as the distribution itself: a package installs **as root** and can run scripts during installation.
+
+## Troubleshooting
+- Read the exact error before retrying anything.
+- Check network reachability and the repository URLs in `/etc/apt/sources.list` and `/etc/apt/sources.list.d/`.
+- Never delete APT/dpkg lock files while a package process is running; find the process (`ps aux | grep -E 'apt|dpkg'`) and let it finish.
+- Do not mix repositories from different releases (a PPA built for 22.04 on a 24.04 system) or, worse, from other distributions.
+- If an interrupted operation left the database inconsistent, follow the distribution's recovery documentation rather than guessing at repair commands.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal (a VM for installs). Try every task before opening its solution.
+

+If `apt search` finds nothing, the indexes were stale; `update` is the fix.

+**Q1.** You removed a package and reinstalled it a month later; your old settings were still there. Why, and how would you have prevented it?

+<details>

+`apt remove` leaves configuration files in `/etc`. `apt purge` removes those too.

+**Q2.** Why is `dpkg -i random-file.deb` riskier than `apt install something`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`dpkg -i` does not resolve dependencies and, more importantly, the file's origin is on you: packages install as root and can execute maintainer scripts. APT installs only from configured, signature-verified repositories.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Which command tells you every file a package owns, and which tells you what package owns a file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Search for `tree`, inspect its metadata, install it, and verify its path with `command -v tree`.
-- Remove it after practice if you do not need it.
-- Hint: package indexes must be current for accurate search results.
+`dpkg -L package` lists a package's files; `dpkg -S /path` finds the owning package.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Confusing `apt update` with `apt upgrade`.
-- Using `dpkg -i` and assuming dependencies will be automatically resolved.
-- Adding untrusted repositories to obtain a single tool.
+- Expecting `dpkg -i` to fetch dependencies.
+- Adding untrusted repositories to get one tool.
+- Killing lock files while a package operation runs.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Use APT for normal package operations and dpkg for lower-level package database tasks.
+APT for repository operations, dpkg for database questions. `update` then `upgrade`, `purge` when you mean "completely", and trust only signed repositories.
 
 ## Further practice
-Read `man apt`, `man dpkg`, and Ubuntu's repository documentation.
+Read `man apt` and `man dpkg`, then inspect three packages on your system: what they own, what owns them, and when they were last upgraded (`grep` the dpkg log).

 ## Learning objectives
-- Identify the package manager for RPM and Arch systems.
-- Install, update, remove, search, and inspect packages.
-- Avoid mixing package ecosystems.
+- Identify the right package manager for RPM-family and Arch systems.
+- Install, update, remove, search, and inspect packages on each.
+- Avoid the classic cross-family and partial-upgrade mistakes.
 
-## Fedora and Red Hat family
+## Fedora and the Red Hat family
 ```bash
-sudo dnf check-update
+sudo dnf check-update     # list available updates (exit 100 means: yes, updates exist)
 sudo dnf search htop
 sudo dnf info htop
 sudo dnf install htop
-sudo dnf upgrade
+sudo dnf upgrade          # the full system upgrade
 sudo dnf remove htop
-rpm -q htop
-rpm -ql bash
-rpm -qf /usr/bin/bash
+rpm -q htop               # installed version
+rpm -ql bash              # files the package owns
+rpm -qf /usr/bin/bash     # package owning this file
 ```
-`dnf` resolves repositories and dependencies. `rpm` queries and manages the RPM package database and local RPM files. `dnf check-update` returning a nonzero status can indicate updates are available; read the output rather than treating every nonzero status as a failure.
+
+The split mirrors Debian's: **dnf** is the high-level manager (repositories, dependencies, upgrades), **rpm** is the low-level database and `.rpm` file tool. Two dnf habits differ from APT habits:
+
+- There is no separate "update the indexes" command; dnf refreshes metadata automatically as needed.
+- `dnf check-update` uses its exit status to communicate: `0` means no updates, `100` means updates are available. A nonzero status here is information, not an error.
+
+RHEL, CentOS Stream, Rocky, and AlmaLinux all share this toolchain, which is why it is the enterprise standard you will meet at work.
 
 ## Arch Linux
 ```bash
-sudo pacman -Syu
-pacman -Ss htop
-sudo pacman -S htop
-pacman -Qi htop
-pacman -Ql htop
-sudo pacman -R htop
+sudo pacman -Syu          # sync databases AND upgrade everything
+pacman -Ss htop           # search repositories
+sudo pacman -S htop       # install
+pacman -Qi htop           # info on an installed package
+pacman -Ql htop           # files the package owns
+pacman -Qo /usr/bin/htop  # package owning this file
+sudo pacman -R htop       # remove
 ```
-`-Syu` synchronizes package databases and performs a full system upgrade. Arch generally expects full upgrades; partial upgrades are unsupported and can break dependency consistency. `-Ss` searches repositories; `-Qi` inspects an installed package; `-Ql` lists package files.
+
+pacman's flags are dense but consistent: capital letters select the operation (`-S` sync/install, `-R` remove, `-Q` query the local database), lowercase letters modify it (`-y` refresh, `-u` upgrade, `-s` search, `-i` info, `-l` list).
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> Arch expects **full** upgrades. Installing one new package without upgrading the rest (`pacman -S something` on a stale system) can pull new libraries into an old system and break dependencies. The supported routine is `pacman -Syu`, then install.
 
 ## Package verification and trust
-Use official repositories and verify package signatures using the distribution's configured trust chain. Do not bypass signature checks to force an installation. Confirm a package's origin and maintainer when auditing a system.
+All three families verify package signatures against keys the distribution configured; that verification is what makes repository software trustworthy. Never bypass signature checks to force an install (`--nogpgcheck`, `SigLevel = Never`): the error is telling you something real. When auditing a system, confirm that every package traces back to an expected repository.
+

+**Q2.** Why is a partial upgrade dangerous on Arch specifically?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Arch is rolling: packages are built against the current library versions. Installing new packages onto otherwise-old libraries mixes incompatible versions and breaks dependencies. `pacman -Syu` keeps the whole system on one consistent snapshot.
+</details>

+<details>

+Stop and investigate the signature error: it may indicate a misconfigured keyring, a wrong repository, or tampering. Disabling verification removes the only check standing between you and an unsigned package running as root.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Search by filename, type, size, owner, permissions, and time.
-- Compare live filesystem search with an indexed search.
+- Search the filesystem by name, type, size, owner, permissions, and time.
+- Choose correctly between live search (find) and indexed search (locate).
 - Handle unusual filenames safely.
 
-## Commands
+## find: the live search
+`find` walks the filesystem **right now**, testing every entry against your predicates:
+
 ```bash
 find ~/linux-lab -type f -name '*.log'
 find /var/log -type f -size +10M 2>/dev/null
@@ -14,48 +16,139 @@ find /home -type f -user "$USER" -mtime -7 2>/dev/null
 find / -xdev -type f -perm -0002 -print 2>/dev/null
 find / -xdev -type f -perm -4000 -print 2>/dev/null
 ```
-`find` traverses the filesystem at execution time. Common predicates:
-- `-name` matches a filename pattern.
-- `-type f` regular file; `-type d` directory; `-type l` symlink.
-- `-size +10M` larger than 10 MiB (GNU find syntax).
-- `-user NAME` owner match.
-- `-mtime -7` modified within the last seven 24-hour periods.
-- `-perm -4000` has SUID bit set.
-- `-xdev` avoids crossing filesystem boundaries.
-- `2>/dev/null` hides permission-denied diagnostics; use it only when you understand that errors are being suppressed.
-
-Other tools:
+
+The predicate kit:
+
+- `-name '*.log'`: filename pattern (quote it, or the shell expands the `*` first).
+- `-type f` regular file, `-type d` directory, `-type l` symlink.
+- `-size +10M` larger than 10 MiB; `-size -1k` smaller than 1 KiB.
+- `-user NAME` owner; `-group NAME` group.
+- `-mtime -7` modified within the last seven 24-hour periods; `-mtime +30` older than thirty.
+- `-perm -4000` has the SUID bit; `-perm -0002` is world-writable.
+- `-xdev` stay on one filesystem (skips `/proc`, mounted disks).
+- `2>/dev/null` hides permission-denied noise. Use it knowingly: it also hides directories the search *could not enter*, so the result is "everything I was allowed to see", not "everything".
+
+Predicates combine with AND by default; `-o` is OR, `!` negates: `find . -type f ! -name '*.txt'`.
+
+`find` can also **act**: `-delete` (dangerous, test with `-print` first) and `-exec cmd {} \;` (run a command per match).
+
+## locate, whereis, which: the fast answers
 ```bash
-locate ssh_config
-whereis ssh
-which python3
-grep -Rni 'Listen' /etc/ssh
+locate ssh_config     # indexed filename search: instant
+whereis ssh           # binary, source, and man page locations
+which python3         # what PATH resolves this name to
+command -v python3    # the script-safe version of which
 ```
-`locate` searches a database and can miss recently created files until its database is updated. `updatedb` refreshes that database and may require elevated privileges; it is not installed or scheduled identically everywhere. `whereis` searches standard locations for binaries, source, and manual pages. `which` searches PATH; `command -v` is often better in shell scripts.
+
+`locate` searches a **database**, not the disk, which makes it instant and stale: a file created after the last database update is invisible until `updatedb` runs (usually scheduled nightly; may need sudo to run manually). Reach for `locate` when speed matters and yesterday is close enough; reach for `find` when correctness matters.
 
 ## Safe filename handling
+Filenames may contain spaces, tabs, even newlines, which breaks naive parsing:
+
 ```bash
 find . -type f -print0 | xargs -0 -r file
 ```
-NUL delimiters safely represent filenames containing spaces, tabs, or newlines.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Create files of different sizes and extensions in `~/linux-lab`.
-- Find files larger than 1 KiB and files modified in the last day.
-- Find world-writable files inside your lab directory.
-- Hint: use `-size +1k` and `-perm -0002`.
+`-print0` separates results with NUL bytes (the only character a filename can never contain) and `xargs -0` reads them the same way. Any pipeline that consumes `find` output for real work should use this pair.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in your own terminal. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** In `~/linux-lab`, create files of different sizes and types, then find (a) every file larger than 1 KiB, (b) everything modified in the last day, (c) every directory.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+cd ~/linux-lab
+printf 'x%.0s' {1..2000} > big.txt
+touch small.txt
+mkdir -p subdir
+find . -type f -size +1k
+find . -mtime -1
+find . -type d
+```
+
+`{1..2000}` is brace expansion producing 2000 arguments, a quick way to make a file with known size.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Find every world-writable file anywhere in your home directory, suppressing only the errors you understand.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+find ~ -type f -perm -0002 -print 2>/dev/null
+```
+
+Each result deserves a look: world-writable inside a home directory is rarely intended. Remember the suppression means unreadable directories were silently skipped.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** `locate notes.txt` finds nothing, but you created the file ten minutes ago. Explain, fix it, and prove both search tools can now find it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+The `locate` database has not been rebuilt since the file was created. Refresh it (may need sudo and take a minute):
+
+```bash
+sudo updatedb
+locate notes.txt
+find ~ -name notes.txt
+```
+
+`find` would have found it all along, because it searches live.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** Print the real type of every file in the current directory, robust against filenames containing spaces.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+find . -maxdepth 1 -type f -print0 | xargs -0 -r file
+```
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why must you quote `'*.log'` in `find . -name '*.log'`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Unquoted, the shell expands `*` against the *current directory* before `find` runs, so `find` receives literal filenames instead of the pattern. Quoted, the pattern reaches `find` intact and is applied at every level of the tree.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** When is `locate` the wrong tool even though it is faster?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+When the file may have been created or moved since the last database update, or when you need attribute tests (size, permissions, time) that the index does not store.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** What does `-xdev` protect against during `find /`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Crossing into other mounted filesystems: the virtual ones (`/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`) full of pseudo-files, and any mounted disks or network shares you did not mean to crawl.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Expecting `locate` to be immediately current.
-- Running `find /` without considering permissions and system load.
+- Expecting `locate` to be current.
+- Running `find /` without `-xdev`, a permission plan, or patience.
 - Suppressing all errors and assuming the search was complete.
 - Parsing `find` output line-by-line when filenames can contain newlines.
+- Forgetting to quote patterns.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Use `find` for live, attribute-based searches. Treat indexed results and suppressed errors carefully.
+`find` for live, attribute-based, correct search; `locate` for instant, slightly stale name lookup; `-print0 | xargs -0` for anything filenames might break.

-Read `man find`, `man locate`, and `man xargs`.
+Read `man find`, `man locate`, and `man xargs`, then write one `find` command that answers a question you actually had this week ("which files in Downloads are over 100M?").
