 ## Learning objectives
-- Identify risky file permissions for review.
-- Understand why credentials and configuration files require protection.
-- Document findings without changing the system.
+- Find risky file permissions worth reviewing.
+- Explain why credentials and configuration files require protection.
+- Document findings properly, without changing the system mid-audit.
+
+## What an audit looks for
+A defensive filesystem review searches for mismatches between how permissions *are* and how they *should be*:
 
-## What to inspect
-A basic defensive review may look for:
 - World-writable files and directories outside intended shared locations.
-- Unexpected SUID/SGID executables.
-- Sensitive files readable by unintended users.
+- Unexpected SUID/SGID executables (module 06 lesson 3).
+- Sensitive files readable by unintended users: private keys, configuration with credentials.
 - Unexpected ownership under application and configuration directories.
-- Private SSH keys with overly broad permissions.
 - Secrets accidentally committed to source trees or written into logs.
 
-Examples:
+## Discovery commands
 ```bash
-find /etc -type f -perm -0002 -ls 2>/dev/null
-find / -xdev -type f -perm -4000 -ls 2>/dev/null
-find /home -type f -name 'id_*' -ls 2>/dev/null
-stat ~/.ssh ~/.ssh/authorized_keys 2>/dev/null
+find /etc -type f -perm -0002 -ls 2>/dev/null          # world-writable config
+find / -xdev -type f -perm -4000 -ls 2>/dev/null       # SUID executables
+find /home -type f -name 'id_*' -ls 2>/dev/null        # private key files
+stat ~/.ssh ~/.ssh/authorized_keys 2>/dev/null         # SSH permission check
+grep -RniE 'password|secret|api[_-]?key' ~/project 2>/dev/null   # secrets in a source tree
+```
+
+Read these as **discovery**, not verdicts. Every result is a question, not an answer: some applications legitimately need special permissions, and `/tmp` is world-writable by design. The audit skill is interpreting each finding in context.
+
+A finding is properly documented before anything is touched:
+
+```text
+Path:        /home/deploy/app/config.yml
+Observed:    -rw-rw-r-- deploy deploy (world-readable, contains a DB password)
+Expected:    -rw-r----- deploy deploy, or a secrets manager
+Risk:        any local account can read production credentials
+Recommendation: chmod 640/o-rwx, move the secret to a managed store, rotate it
 ```
-These are discovery commands, not proof of a vulnerability. Interpret findings in context. Some applications legitimately need special permissions. Do not alter system-owned files without a change plan.
 
 ### Protecting secrets
-- Store private keys with restrictive permissions (commonly `600` for a private key file).
-- Limit access to configuration files containing credentials.
-- Avoid putting passwords in shell history, command-line arguments, or source code.
-- Use a secrets manager or protected service configuration where appropriate.
-- Rotate credentials if exposure is suspected.
-
-## Hands-on practice
-- Audit only `~/linux-lab`, not the entire host.
-- Create one deliberately world-readable sample file and one private sample file.
-- Compare `stat` output and explain the access difference.
-- Write a short finding with path, owner, mode, expected state, and recommendation.
+- Private keys: mode `600`, owned by the user. SSH clients refuse keys with sloppy permissions.
+- Configuration with credentials: mode `640` or tighter, owned by the service's account and group.
+- Keep secrets out of shell history, command arguments (visible in `ps`), and source code.
+- Suspected exposure means **rotation**, not just re-permissioning: whoever read the secret already has it.
+
+## The auditor's discipline
+1. Record the original state (`stat`, `ls -l`, package ownership) **before** any change.
+2. Never alter system-owned files without a change plan and a rollback path.
+3. Never run an untrusted "hardening script" with root privileges to fix findings for you.
+4. A scan result is never proof of exploitability; it is a prioritization list.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Do these in `~/linux-lab` only, not against the whole host. Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Create one deliberately world-readable "secret" file and one correctly protected private key, then compare them with `stat`.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+cd ~/linux-lab
+printf 'DB_PASS=hunter2\n' > config.yml
+chmod 644 config.yml
+printf 'FAKE-PRIVATE-KEY\n' > id_test
+chmod 600 id_test
+stat -c '%a %U:%G %n' config.yml id_test
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+644 learner:learner config.yml
+600 learner:learner id_test
+```
+
+Any local account can read `config.yml`; only the owner can read `id_test`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Audit your lab directory for world-writable and world-readable files, and write one properly formatted finding for the worst offender.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+find ~/linux-lab -type f -perm -0002 -ls 2>/dev/null
+find ~/linux-lab -type f -perm -0004 -ls 2>/dev/null
+```
+
+Then write the finding in the four-field format from this lesson: path, observed state, expected state, risk, recommendation. The `-0004` test catches world-*readable* files, which matters when the content is sensitive.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Check your own SSH setup's permissions and fix anything too broad.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+stat -c '%a %n' ~/.ssh ~/.ssh/* 2>/dev/null
+```
+
+Expected: `~/.ssh` at `700`, private keys (`id_*` without `.pub`) at `600`, public keys and `authorized_keys` at `644` or tighter. Fix with `chmod 700 ~/.ssh && chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_*`.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 4.** Hunt for an accidentally committed secret in a project directory without exposing it further.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+grep -RniE 'password|secret|api[_-]?key|BEGIN .*PRIVATE KEY' ~/linux-lab 2>/dev/null
+```
+
+If anything matches a *real* credential: rotate the credential first, then remove it from the file, then scrub history if it was committed to git. Re-permissioning alone does not undo exposure.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why is "world-readable" not automatically a finding?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Plenty of files are world-readable by design: documentation, public web content, `/etc/os-release`. A finding requires sensitive *content* plus broad *access*; the audit's job is judging both.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What are the first two actions after discovering a real password in a world-readable file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Rotate the password (assume it is compromised), then fix the file's permissions and move the secret to proper storage. Order matters: fixing permissions first leaves the exposed credential valid.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Why record the original state before changing anything?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Evidence and rollback: you need to prove what you found, and you need a way back if the "fix" breaks the application.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
 - Calling every world-readable file sensitive.
-- Treating a scan result as proof of exploitability.
+- Treating scan output as proof of exploitability.
 - Changing permissions before recording the original state.
-- Running untrusted scripts with root privileges to “fix” findings.
+- Running untrusted fix-it scripts as root.

 ## Introduction
-SSH (Secure Shell) provides encrypted remote login and command execution. It uses a client-server model: the client connects to an SSH server, commonly on TCP port 22, though servers can use another port.
+SSH (Secure Shell) provides encrypted remote login and command execution. A client connects to an SSH server, conventionally on TCP port 22. Nearly all remote Linux administration happens over SSH, and nearly all of it authenticates two ways: the server proves its identity to you (host key), and you prove yours to it (password or, better, a user key, covered in the next lesson).
 
 ## Connection syntax
 ```bash
@@ -16,15 +16,32 @@ ssh -p 2222 username@192.0.2.20
 ssh -v username@localhost
 ssh -o ConnectTimeout=5 username@host.example
 ```
-`-p` selects the port; `-v` prints diagnostic detail; `-o` sets a client option. Documentation address `192.0.2.0/24` is reserved for examples and is not a real target.
 
-### Host-key verification
-The server presents a host key to identify itself. SSH records accepted keys in `~/.ssh/known_hosts`. If the key changes unexpectedly, SSH warns because the server may have been rebuilt, its key rotated, DNS may point elsewhere, or a man-in-the-middle attack may be occurring.
+- `-p` selects the port (client option; the server sets its port in its own configuration).
+- `-v` prints negotiation detail; add more v's (`-vvv`) for deeper debugging.
+- `-o` sets any client option inline.
 
-Do not blindly disable verification with `StrictHostKeyChecking=no` or delete a warning without checking through a trusted channel. Confirm the fingerprint with the system owner or administrator.
+(`192.0.2.0/24` is a documentation range reserved for examples, not a real target.)
+
+## Host-key verification: the prompt that protects you
+On first connection, the server presents its **host key**, and the client asks you to confirm its fingerprint:
+
+```text
+The authenticity of host '192.0.2.20' can't be established.
+ED25519 key fingerprint is SHA256:AbCdEf123...
+Are you sure you want to continue connecting (yes/no/[fingerprint])?
+```
+
+Accept, and the key is recorded in `~/.ssh/known_hosts`. On every later connection the client checks the presented key against that record **silently**. The design assumes the first check is done carefully (ideally against a fingerprint you received through a trusted channel: the console, the cloud dashboard, the administrator).
+
+If the key ever **changes**, SSH stops you with a loud warning. The innocent explanations: the server was rebuilt, its keys were rotated, or DNS now points elsewhere. The hostile explanation: someone is impersonating the server (a man-in-the-middle attack). You cannot tell which from the warning alone, so verify through a trusted channel before proceeding.
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> Never silence these warnings with `StrictHostKeyChecking=no`, and never just delete the `known_hosts` line and reconnect. Both train you to click through the one defense against server impersonation.
+
+## Client configuration
+Frequent connections belong in `~/.ssh/config`:
 
-## Configuration
-Client configuration is commonly stored in `~/.ssh/config`:
 ```sshconfig
 Host lab-vm
     HostName 192.0.2.20
@@ -32,31 +49,108 @@ Host lab-vm
     Port 2222
     IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
 ```
-Protect the file from unintended modification; `chmod 600 ~/.ssh/config` is a common setting.
 
-## Troubleshooting
-- `Connection timed out`: routing, firewall, address, or host availability.
-- `Connection refused`: host reachable but no listener at that port, or actively rejected.
-- `Permission denied`: username, authentication method, account policy, or key permissions.
-- Host-key warning: verify identity before proceeding.
+Now `ssh lab-vm` expands the whole block. Protect the file: `chmod 600 ~/.ssh/config`.
+
+## Troubleshooting: the four classic failures
+| Message | What it actually means |
+|---|---|
+| `Connection timed out` | Packets are dying on the way: wrong address, routing, or a firewall silently dropping them. |
+| `Connection refused` | The host is reachable, but nothing listens on that port (server down, wrong port) or it actively rejected you. |
+| `Permission denied (publickey...)` | You reached a working server; *authentication* failed: wrong user, wrong key, or key not authorized. |
+| Host-key warning | Identity mismatch; verify before doing anything else. |
+
+Read the message, name the layer (network, service, authentication, identity), and only then start changing things.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Generate and use an SSH key pair.
-- Explain public versus private keys.
-- Transfer files with SCP and SFTP.
+- Generate and use an SSH key pair, and explain the public/private split precisely.
+- Load keys into ssh-agent so passphrases stop interrupting your day.
+- Transfer files with SCP and SFTP, and apply the secure-server checklist.
 
 ## Key authentication
-A key pair has a **private key** kept secret on your client and a **public key** placed in the remote account's `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. During authentication, the client proves possession of the private key without sending it to the server.
+A key pair is two mathematically linked files:
+
+- The **private key** stays secret on your client machine, protected by its permissions and (ideally) a passphrase. Anyone who copies it *becomes* you.
+- The **public key** is placed on servers, in the remote account's `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. It can be shared freely; deriving the private half from it is computationally infeasible.
+
+During authentication the server issues a challenge that only the private key can answer; the private key itself never crosses the network. That is the whole trick, and it is why key authentication beats passwords: nothing reusable is ever transmitted, and guessing is out of reach.
+
+Generate a modern Ed25519 pair:
 
-Generate a modern Ed25519 key:
 ```bash
 ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "learner@lab"
 ```
-Use a passphrase where practical. The private key is commonly stored under `~/.ssh/id_ed25519`; the public key ends in `.pub`. Never share the private key.
 
-Copy the public key to a server you administer:
+Output:
+
+```text
+Generating public/private ed25519 key pair.
+Enter file in which to save the key (/home/learner/.ssh/id_ed25519):
+Enter passphrase (empty for no passphrase):
+```
+
+Use a passphrase. It encrypts the private key at rest, so a stolen laptop or leaked backup does not hand over your identity. The private key lands at `~/.ssh/id_ed25519` (mode `600`), the public half at `~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub`.
+
+Deploy the public key to a server you administer:
+
 ```bash
 ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub learner@192.0.2.20
 ```
-`ssh-copy-id` may not be installed on every platform. Alternatively, append the public key through a secure, verified setup process.
 
-### ssh-agent
+`ssh-copy-id` appends to `authorized_keys` with the right permissions (not available on every platform; appending manually works if done carefully: **append**, never overwrite, or you lock out every previously installed key, including possibly your own).
+
+## ssh-agent: type the passphrase once
 ```bash
 eval "$(ssh-agent -s)"
 ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
-ssh-add -l
+ssh-add -l      # list loaded identities
+ssh-add -D      # remove all when done
 ```
-The agent holds decrypted key material in memory for a session. Protect your account and remove keys from the agent when no longer needed (`ssh-add -D` removes all identities from the agent).
 
-### File transfer
+The agent holds the decrypted key in memory for your session, so each SSH connection authenticates without re-asking. Many desktop sessions start an agent for you; `ssh-add -l` tells you whether one is running and what it holds.
+
+## File transfer
 ```bash
-scp report.txt learner@192.0.2.20:/home/learner/
-scp learner@192.0.2.20:/var/log/example.log ./example.log
-sftp learner@192.0.2.20
+scp report.txt learner@192.0.2.20:/home/learner/        # upload
+scp learner@192.0.2.20:/var/log/example.log ./          # download
+scp -r project/ learner@192.0.2.20:/home/learner/       # directories
+sftp learner@192.0.2.20                                 # interactive session
 ```
-SCP copies files over SSH. SFTP provides an interactive file-transfer protocol; common commands include `get`, `put`, `ls`, and `exit`.
+
+SCP is a copy command over SSH: same syntax instincts as `cp`, with `host:path` for the remote side. SFTP is an interactive transfer shell (`get`, `put`, `ls`, `cd`, `exit`) for browsing before moving files. Both ride the same encrypted channel and the same keys as your logins; never fall back to plain FTP.
 
 ## Secure server principles
-- Prefer key-based authentication and strong account controls.
-- Keep OpenSSH updated.
-- Restrict access using firewall rules and account policy.
-- Disable password authentication only after confirming key access and recovery.
-- Avoid direct root login where practical.
-- Keep host-key checking enabled.
+When the server is yours to harden:
+
+- Prefer key-based authentication; keep OpenSSH updated.
+- Restrict who can even reach the port (firewall rules, security groups).
+- Disable password authentication **only after** confirming key login works in a second session and you have a recovery path.
+- Disable direct root login (`PermitRootLogin no`).
+- Keep host-key checking enabled everywhere.
+
+Server options live in `/etc/ssh/sshd_config` (plus included files; defaults vary by distribution). Validate with `sudo sshd -t` before restarting, and keep one working session open while you test from a new one.
+

 ## Learning objectives
-- Create and execute a Bash script.
-- Use variables, command substitution, arguments, and `read`.
-- Understand common special parameters.
+- Create and execute a Bash script with a proper shebang.
+- Use variables, command substitution, arguments, and `read` correctly.
+- Know the special parameters and quote everything that needs quoting.
 
-## First script
+## Your first script
 Create `hello.sh`:
+
 ```bash
 #!/usr/bin/env bash
 set -u
 
 name="${1:-learner}"
-printf 'Hello, %s!
-' "$name"
+printf 'Hello, %s!\n' "$name"
+```
+
+Three lines of ceremony, each earning its place:
+
+- `#!/usr/bin/env bash` is the **shebang**: it tells the kernel which interpreter runs this file. `env` finds `bash` through `PATH`, which is more portable than a hardcoded path.
+- `set -u` makes referencing an unset variable an error instead of a silent empty string. Typos fail loudly instead of corrupting paths.
+- `${1:-learner}` is a default: the first argument if set and non-empty, otherwise `learner`.
+
+Run it two ways:
+
+```bash
+chmod +x hello.sh
+./hello.sh Yaoguai
+bash hello.sh
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+Hello, Yaoguai!
+Hello, learner!
 ```
-The shebang selects Bash through `env`. `set -u` treats unset variables as errors. `${1:-learner}` uses the first argument if set and non-empty, otherwise a default. Make it executable with `chmod +x hello.sh`, then run `./hello.sh Yaoguai`. You can also run `bash hello.sh Yaoguai`.
 
-### Variables and quoting
+`chmod +x` plus the shebang makes it a real program; `bash hello.sh` skips both by handing the file to Bash directly.
+
+## Variables and quoting
 ```bash
 project='Linux Essentials'
-printf '%s
-' "$project"
+printf '%s\n' "$project"
 kernel="$(uname -r)"
-printf 'Kernel: %s
-' "$kernel"
+printf 'Kernel: %s\n' "$kernel"
 ```
-No spaces are allowed around `=` in a simple assignment. Quote expansions to prevent word splitting and glob expansion.
 
-### Parameters
+Rules that break scripts when ignored:
+
+- **No spaces around `=`.** `name = value` is not an assignment; Bash parses it as running a command called `name` with arguments.
+- **Quote expansions**: `"$project"`. Unquoted, the value is word-split and glob-expanded (module 04 lesson 1), so a variable holding `my dir` silently becomes two arguments.
+- `$(command)` captures a command's stdout into the variable.
+
+## Special parameters
 | Parameter | Meaning |
 |---|---|
-| `$0` | Script name |
-| `$1`, `$2` | Positional arguments |
-| `$@` | All arguments as separate words when quoted as `"$@"` |
-| `$#` | Number of positional arguments |
-| `$?` | Previous command's exit status |
-| `$$` | Current shell process ID |
+| `$0` | Script name as invoked |
+| `$1`, `$2`, ... | Positional arguments |
+| `$#` | How many arguments were given |
+| `"$@"` | All arguments, each preserved as its own word |
+| `$?` | Exit status of the last command |
+| `$$` | The script's own process ID |
 
-Use `"$@"` when forwarding arguments. Avoid `$*` for most argument-forwarding tasks because it joins arguments.
+Forward arguments to another program with `"$@"`, never `$*` (which mushes them into one string) and never unquoted `$@` (which splits them on spaces). `"$@"` is the only form that treats `deploy.sh "my server" prod` as two arguments, which it is.
 
-### Input and exit codes
+## Input and exit codes
 ```bash
 read -r -p 'Enter a hostname: ' host
-printf 'You entered: %s
-' "$host"
+printf 'You entered: %s\n' "$host"
 exit 0
 ```
-`read -r` prevents backslash interpretation. Exit status 0 conventionally means success; nonzero means failure. `exit` terminates the script.
 
-## Hands-on practice
-- Write a script that accepts a first name and prints a greeting.
-- Add a check that exactly one argument was supplied.
-- Try an argument containing spaces.
-- Hint: use `[[ $# -eq 1 ]]` and quote `"$1"`.
+- `read -r` stops backslashes being mangled; make `-r` a reflex.
+- Exit status is your script's report to whoever ran it: `0` means success, anything else means failure. `exit 3` tells a calling script (or a human checking `$?`) not just *that* you failed but *how*.
+
+## Practice exercises
+Try every task before opening its solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Write `greet.sh`: takes exactly one argument (a name), prints `Hello, NAME!`, and exits with status 2 and a usage message if the argument count is wrong.

+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>

+```bash
+#!/usr/bin/env bash

+

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why does `name = Ada` fail, and what does Bash think you meant?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+With spaces it is not an assignment: Bash parses `name` as a command with arguments `=` and `Ada`, and reports "name: command not found". Assignments are exactly `name=value`.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What does `set -u` catch that Bash would otherwise ignore?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+References to unset variables, which otherwise expand to empty strings. `rm -r "$dir/"` with a typo'd `$dri` is the classic disaster `set -u` turns into a clean error.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** Why is `"$@"` the only safe way to forward all arguments?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Quoted, it expands to each argument as a separate word, spaces inside arguments intact. Unquoted `$@` splits on spaces; `"$*"` glues everything into one word. Both corrupt multi-word arguments.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Forgetting the shebang.
-- Writing `name = value` with spaces.
-- Using unquoted variables in paths.
-- Using `echo` for output that requires predictable escape handling; prefer `printf`.
+- Forgetting the shebang, then watching `sh` run Bash syntax and fail strangely.
+- Spaces around `=`.
+- Unquoted variables in paths.
+- Using `echo` for output where escapes and leading dashes matter; `printf` is predictable.
+- Exiting without a status, leaving callers unable to tell success from failure.

-Bash scripts are programs. Quote variable expansions, validate input, and return meaningful exit statuses.
+Scripts are programs: declare the interpreter, fail loudly on unset variables, quote expansions, validate input, and return honest exit codes.

-## Tests
+## Tests: asking questions
 ```bash
-[[ -f "$path" ]]   # regular file exists
-[[ -d "$path" ]]   # directory exists

+[[ -d "$path" ]]          # directory exists

+[[ -e "$path" ]]          # exists at all

+[[ "$name" == ad* ]]      # pattern match
+[[ -n "$value" ]]         # non-empty string

-Bash `[[ ... ]]` is a Bash keyword and safer/more expressive than the historical `[ ... ]` in several cases. `test` and `[ ... ]` are widely available but have stricter quoting and parsing rules. For POSIX shell portability, use POSIX-compatible tests.
+
+Use Bash's `[[ ... ]]`, not the older `[ ... ]`: inside `[[ ]]` word splitting and glob expansion are disabled, so quoted-empty variables and spaces behave. (The classic `[ $x = y ]` fails outright when `$x` is empty; `[[ $x == y ]]` does not.) Note the two families: `==` compares strings, `-eq` compares numbers; mixing them compares the wrong things.
 
 ## Conditionals and case
 ```bash

-    printf 'File exists
-'
+    printf 'File exists\n'

-    printf 'Directory exists

+    printf 'Directory exists\n'

+`case` is the cleaner tool when one value drives many branches, and every service-style script uses this shape:
+
+```bash

-    start) printf 'Starting

-    stop)  printf 'Stopping

+    stop)   printf 'Stopping\n' ;;
+    status) printf 'Checking\n' ;;
+    *)      printf 'Usage: %s {start|stop|status}\n' "$0" >&2; exit 2 ;;

 ```
 
+`"${1:-}"` supplies an empty default so an absent argument lands in the usage branch instead of erroring under `set -u`; `*` is the catch-all.

 ## Loops
 ```bash

-`while` repeats while its condition succeeds; `until` repeats until its condition succeeds. Avoid busy-wait loops without a delay.

+- `for` iterates a fixed list or a glob. Globbing (`*.log`) is the safe way to loop over files.
+- `while IFS= read -r line` is **the** correct file-reading idiom: `IFS=` preserves leading/trailing whitespace, `-r` preserves backslashes. Never use `for x in $(cat file)`: it splits on every whitespace character and expands globs, mangling real data.
+- `while` repeats while its condition succeeds; `until` repeats until it does. Any wait loop needs a `sleep` or it burns a CPU core busy-spinning.

-## Arithmetic and strings
+Functions take positional parameters just like scripts (`$1`, `$2`), and `local` keeps their variables from leaking into the rest of the script. Without `local`, a function that assigns `name=...` silently overwrites a global of the same name, one of the sneakiest Bash bugs.
+
+Arrays hold lists without packing them into strings. `"${array[@]}"` (quoted, with `@`) expands to each element as its own word; every other form mangles elements containing spaces.
+
+## Arithmetic

-Arithmetic expansion uses `$(( ... ))`. `-n` tests non-empty strings; `-z` tests empty strings.

+found=0
+for file in *.jpeg; do
+    found=1
+    mv -- "$file" "${file%.jpeg}.jpg"

+done
+(( found )) || printf 'no .jpeg files here\n'
+```
+
+`nullglob` makes the glob expand to nothing (instead of the literal text `*.jpeg`) when there are no matches; `${file%.jpeg}` strips the suffix.

+    list)
+        [[ -f "$file" ]] && cat -- "$file" || printf 'no notes yet\n'

+    *)

+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Why does `for f in $(ls)` break, and what replaces it?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`$(ls)` output is split on every whitespace character and glob-expanded, so `my file.txt` becomes two broken words. Loop the glob directly: `for f in *` (optionally with `nullglob`).
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What is the difference between `[[ $a == 3 ]]` and `[[ $a -eq 3 ]]` when `$a` is `03`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`==` compares strings: `03` does not equal `3`. `-eq` compares numbers: 3 equals 3. Choose by what the data is, not what it looks like.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** A function sets `count=5` and afterwards the script's own `count` changed. Why, and the one-word fix?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Bash variables are global by default, so the function overwrote the script's variable. Fix: declare it `local count` inside the function.
+</details>

 ```bash

-- `-e`: exit on many unhandled command failures.
+
+- `-e`: exit when a command fails.
 - `-u`: treat unset variables as errors.

-These options are useful but **not a complete error-handling system**. `-e` has exceptions in conditionals, `&&`/`||` lists, pipelines, and functions. Explicitly check commands whose failure matters. Initialize optional variables and handle expected nonzero statuses.
+These catch whole categories of bugs, but they are **not a complete error-handling system**, and `-e` in particular has a famous list of exceptions: commands inside `if` conditions, `&&`/`||` lists, and pipeline stages are allowed to fail without exiting (that is how conditions work at all). +```bash

+## Input validation: treat input as data
 ```bash

-    printf 'Usage: %s DIRECTORY
-' "$0" >&2
+    printf 'Usage: %s DIRECTORY\n' "$0" >&2

-## Debugging
+Rules that keep scripts safe:
+
+- Validate count, then shape, then existence, in that order.
+- Usage errors exit `2`; operational failures exit `1`; success exits `0`. Consistent codes let callers react correctly.
+- Messages about errors go to stderr (`>&2`), never stdout.
+- **Never** build a command string from input and run it through `eval`, and never pass input to `sh -c`. Use arrays for argument lists and quote every expansion.
+
+## Debugging without leaking
 ```bash
-bash -n script.sh       # syntax check
-bash -x script.sh       # trace commands
+bash -n script.sh     # syntax check only; runs nothing
+bash -x script.sh     # trace: prints each command as expanded
 ```
-`set -x` can reveal secrets expanded into commands; do not enable it around passwords, tokens, or sensitive data. Add meaningful messages to stderr and use appropriate exit codes.
+
+`bash -n` before every first run. `bash -x` when behavior surprises you: the trace shows exactly what each line became after expansion, which is usually where the surprise lives.
+
+> [!WARNING]
+> `bash -x` prints *expanded* commands, including any password or token a variable held. Never trace scripts that handle secrets, and never paste traces into tickets without reading them first.

-`read -r` reads literal input. The Bash regular-expression operator `=~` checks a 1–32 character allowlist. The accepted value is printed, not executed.
+
+Pattern: allowlist validation with `=~` (Bash regex). Define what is *permitted* (1-32 letters, digits, dashes) instead of trying to enumerate what is dangerous.

+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+One argument: the `$# -ne 2` usage guard, exit 2. Nonexistent source: the `[[ -d ]]` guard, exit 1. Path with spaces: works correctly *because* every expansion is quoted; try removing the quotes and watch it fail, then restore them.
+</details>

+wc -l < "$log"
+```
+
+`wc -l < file` prints just the number (no filename), and it never fails the way `grep -c` does on zero matches, so no `|| true` is needed.
+</details>

+**Exercise 4.** Add a `--help` option to the monitoring script (script 6) that prints usage and exits 0.
+

+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>

+
+```bash
+if [[ "${1:-}" == "--help" ]]; then
+    printf 'Usage: %s [INTERVAL_SECONDS]\n' "$0"
+    exit 0
+fi
+```
+
+Note the exit status: asking for help is success, so `0`, not `2`.
+</details>
+
+## Check yourself
+Answer each question, then open the answer to verify.
+
+**Q1.** Under `set -e`, why does `if grep -q x file; then ...` not exit the script when the grep finds nothing?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Commands in a condition context are exempt from `-e`; their status feeds the `if` instead. This exemption is exactly why `-e` alone is not error handling.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** Why is `eval "rm -r $user_input"` catastrophic even when you "trust" the user?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`eval` re-parses the string as shell code, so a value like `x; rm -rf ~` runs the second command too. Mistakes and attacks look identical to `eval`. Keep input in quoted variables; never re-parse it.
+</details>
+
+**Q3.** What is the difference between exit codes 1 and 2 in this lesson's scripts, and why does the distinction matter?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+2 means "you called me wrong" (usage); 1 means "you called me right but the work failed". A wrapper script can then retry operational failures while reporting usage bugs to the developer instead of retrying them forever.
+</details>
 
 ## Common mistakes
-- Assuming strict mode catches every possible error.
-- Using `eval` with user-controlled input.
-- Forgetting to quote paths.
-- Exposing secrets through `bash -x`.
-- Running scripts with `sudo` before testing as a regular user.
+- Assuming strict mode catches everything.
+- `eval` or `sh -c` on user-controlled input.
+- Unquoted paths.
+- Tracing with `bash -x` around secrets.
+- Running new scripts with `sudo` before testing them as a regular user.
+- Backups never tested with a restore.
 
 ## Key takeaways
-Safe scripts validate inputs, quote variables, check important failures, and avoid interpreting untrusted data as commands.
+Validate count, shape, then existence; quote everything; keep input as data; check important failures explicitly even under strict mode. `bash -n` always, `bash -x` carefully.

-Extend the log analyzer to accept a time window or output path, while preserving safe argument handling.
+Run [ShellCheck](https://www.shellcheck.net/) on every script in this lesson and on one of your own; it is the single fastest way to level up shell code.

 ## Learning objectives
-- Explain the principle of least privilege.
-- Recognize common privilege and configuration risks.
-- Build a safe, evidence-based review workflow.
+- Explain least privilege as a daily operating practice, not a slogan.
+- Recognize the common privilege and configuration risks on a real host.
+- Run a safe, evidence-based review workflow on systems you administer.
 
 ## Core concepts
-Linux security is built from multiple controls: identity, permissions, process isolation, kernel security features, network policy, package trust, service configuration, and monitoring. No single permission listing proves a host is secure.
+Linux security is layered: identity, permissions, process isolation, kernel security features, network policy, package trust, service configuration, and monitoring all stack together. No single listing proves a host is secure; security is the *combined* effect, and weakness in one layer is often survivable because of the others. Your job as an administrator is to keep every layer boring and intentional.
+
+Least privilege, as a working routine:
 
-Least privilege means:
 1. Use a regular account for routine work.
-2. Elevate only the command that requires administration.
-3. Give services dedicated accounts and only required access.
+2. Elevate only the command that needs administration, only while it runs.
+3. Give every service a dedicated account with only the access it needs.
 4. Avoid broad sudo rules and shared credentials.
-5. Remove obsolete accounts, keys, and services through an approved process.
+5. Remove obsolete accounts, keys, packages, and services through a controlled process.
+
+## The defensive review toolkit
+Run these on systems you administer or are explicitly authorized to audit:
 
-## Defensive review commands
 ```bash
 id
 sudo -l
@@ -26,31 +29,120 @@ ss -tulpen

 ```
-`sudo -l` shows commands the current user may run under sudo policy. `ss` inspects sockets; `-t` TCP, `-u` UDP, `-l` listening, `-p` process, `-e` extended, `-n` numeric. Some process details require root. These commands are for systems you administer or are authorized to audit.
 
-## Security interpretation
-- A listening port is not automatically a vulnerability; identify the service and exposure.
-- A privileged executable is not automatically exploitable; check package origin and expected behavior.
-- A writable configuration file may allow unauthorized changes; determine who needs write access.
-- A log entry is evidence of an event, not necessarily proof of compromise.
+What each answers:
+
+- `sudo -l`: exactly which commands *this* user may elevate. Broad output (`(ALL) ALL`) on an account that does not need it is a finding.
+- `ss -tulpen`: listening sockets: TCP (`-t`), UDP (`-u`), listening (`-l`), with owning process (`-p`) and no DNS delays (`-n`). Every line is a door into the machine; each should be a door you meant to install. Some process details require root.
+- `getent group sudo` / `getent passwd`: who can administer, and which accounts can log in at all.
+- The SUID `find` from module 06: privileged code on disk.
+
+## Interpreting what you find
+Evidence discipline, learned from painful experience:
+
+- A listening port is not automatically a vulnerability: identify the service, its bind address (`127.0.0.1` versus `0.0.0.0` is the difference between local-only and world-facing), and whether it should exist.
+- A SUID executable is not automatically exploitable: check package origin, updates, and expected behavior.
+- A writable configuration file is a question about who needs write access, not an automatic incident.
+- A log entry is evidence of an event, not proof of compromise. Corroborate before concluding.
+
+## A safe review workflow
+1. **Scope:** confirm you are authorized for this host, and write down what you are reviewing.
+2. **Collect:** identity, sudo rights, listening sockets, running services, SUID files, login-capable accounts.
+3. **Interpret:** for each item, answer "is this intended, documented, and current?"
+4. **Record:** findings with path/port, observed state, expected state, risk, and recommendation.
+5. **Change** through your normal process, one finding at a time, with rollback notes.
+

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Accountability and revocation: shared credentials make every action unattributable in the logs, and removing one person's access means changing the password for everyone. Individual accounts plus group-based sudo give you both.
+</details>

+
+<details>

+</details>

 ## Suggested commands

+Notes on the subtle parts:
+
+- `sudo -u labalice command` runs one command *as* that user, perfect for tests without switching accounts.
+- The `2` in `2770` is the SGID bit (module 06 lesson 3); `770` grants owner and group full access and nothing to others.
+- Group membership changes apply to new login sessions; `sudo -u` starts a fresh context, which sidesteps that for testing.

-The extraction regex is a simple teaching example and does not validate IPv4 octets.
 
-## Expected interpretation

+Build each pipeline one stage at a time and check the output before appending the next stage. The extraction regex matches IP-shaped text; it is not IPv4 validation.

 ## Extension
-Use `awk` to count events by username. Remember that the log's free-form message format makes field positions dependent on the event type.
+Use `awk` to count events by username. Remember that the message format differs per event type, so field positions shift; decide how you will handle `invalid user guest` (two words) before trusting your counts.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```bash
+grep -i 'Failed password' sample-auth.log |
+  sed 's/invalid user //' |
+  awk '{for(i=1;i<=NF;i++) if ($i=="from") {print $(i-1); break}}' |
+  sort | uniq -c | sort -nr
+```
+
+Output:
+
+```text
+      1 test
+      1 root
+      1 guest
+      1 analyst
+```
+
+The `sed` normalizes the two-word "invalid user" case, then `awk` prints the word before `from` on each line.
+</details>

+Submit the commands, the counts, the extracted tokens with their ranking, `failed-events.txt`, and your three-sentence note.

+## Common mistakes
+- Counting with `grep -i` one way and `-c` another and reporting inconsistent totals.
+- Trusting the IP-shaped regex as validation.
+- Editing the sample log mid-analysis; always work on a copy.

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+

+## Background
+Try the obvious first and watch it fail: `cat -` does not read a file named `-`. Nearly every Unix command-line program treats an argument starting with `-` as an **option**, and a bare `-` by convention often means standard input. So `cat -` waits for you to type, which looks exactly like the command "hanging". (If that happens to you, `Ctrl-C` out.)
+
+Two standard escapes exist, and both are worth knowing because this exact problem recurs with files like `-rf`, `--help`, and worse:
+
+- **Make it a path.** A name containing `/` is never parsed as an option: `./-` is unambiguously the file `-` in the current directory.
+- **End the options.** GNU utilities accept `--` to mean "everything after this is an operand, not an option": `cat -- -`.

 ```bash
@@ -15,18 +23,32 @@ cat -- -
 ```

+3. Many GNU utilities accept `--` to end option processing.

+Option parsers split arguments into options and operands before the program sees them. -# Bandit Level 2 to Level 3: Spaces in Filenames
+# Bandit Level 2 to Level 3: Spaces in a Filename
 
 
 ## Level objective
-Read the file identified by the official level page when its name contains spaces.
+Read the file in the home directory whose name contains spaces. Confirm the exact goal on the official level page.
 
 ## Concepts practiced
-Quoting, escaping, and filename completion.
+Word splitting, quoting, and escape characters, the core of module 04 lesson 1 applied to a real annoyance.
+
+## Background
+The file is named `spaces in this filename`. Type it bare and the shell performs **word splitting**: the one name becomes four arguments, and `cat` tries to open four files named `spaces`, `in`, `this`, and `filename`, failing on the first with "No such file or directory".
+
+The shell gives you three equivalent ways to keep the name one argument:
+
+- **Single quotes:** `'spaces in this filename'` — everything literal.
+- **Double quotes:** `"spaces in this filename"` — literal except `$`, backticks, and `\`.
+- **Escaping each space:** `spaces\ in\ this\ filename` — the backslash escapes the character after it.
+
+In practice, nobody types the escapes: type `cat sp` and press **Tab**, and the shell completes the name with the escaping done for you. Completion is the everyday answer; quoting is the scripting answer.
 
 ## Relevant commands
 ```bash
-ls -lb
-cat 'spaces in this filename'
+ls -la
+cat "spaces in this filename"
 cat spaces\ in\ this\ filename
 ```
 
 ## Hints (in increasing order)
-1. The shell normally splits unquoted words at spaces.
-2. Put the complete filename in single quotes.
-3. Alternatively, escape each space with a backslash.
+1. The spaces are splitting the name into separate arguments.
+2. Quoting or escaping keeps the name whole.
+3. Tab completion writes the escapes for you.
 
 ## Solution explanation
-Quote the full filename as one shell argument. Single quotes preserve the spaces literally.
+`ls -la` shows the spaced filename. Read it with any of the quoted or escaped forms above to get the next password, then log in to the next level.
 
 ## Why it works
-The shell performs word splitting before launching `cat`. Quoting prevents the filename from becoming several separate arguments.
+Word splitting happens after the shell reads your line and before the command runs, at every unquoted space. Quoting and escaping both mark the spaces as *data*, so the command receives exactly one argument: the real filename.
+
+## Check yourself
+**Q1.** In a script, a variable holds a filename that might contain spaces. Which form is safe: `cat $file`, `cat "$file"`, or `cat '$file'`?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`cat "$file"`. Unquoted splits the value on spaces; single quotes would print the literal text `$file` instead of the variable's value.
+</details>

-# Bandit Level 8 to Level 9: Find a Unique Line
+# Bandit Level 8 to Level 9: The Line That Appears Once
 
 
 ## Level objective
-Identify the line that occurs only once in the supplied data.
+In `data.txt`, the password is on the only line that occurs exactly once. Find it. Confirm the exact goal on the official level page.
 
 ## Concepts practiced
-Sorting, adjacent duplicate detection, and counting.
+The `sort | uniq` idiom, and *why the sort is mandatory*.
+
+## Background
+The file is full of repeated decoy lines; the real line is unique. `uniq -u` prints lines that occur only once, but with a catch that bites everyone once: `uniq` compares **adjacent** lines only. In an unsorted file, the same line appearing at positions 5 and 9000 looks "unique" at each position. Sorting first gathers all copies of every line together, making `uniq`'s counts globally true.
+
+So the classic pipeline is two stages: `sort` to group, `uniq -u` to keep singletons. Expect the sort of a million-line file to take a moment; that is normal.
 
 ## Relevant commands
 ```bash
 sort data.txt | uniq -u
-sort data.txt | uniq -c
 ```
 
 ## Hints (in increasing order)
-1. The file contains repeated lines.
-2. `uniq` compares adjacent lines, so sort the input first.
-3. `uniq -u` prints lines that occur exactly once in the sorted stream.
+1. Duplicates must stand next to each other to be counted.
+2. `sort` before `uniq`.
+3. `-u` keeps lines seen exactly once.
 
 ## Solution explanation
-Sort the file and pass it to `uniq -u`. Confirm the resulting line matches the official condition.
+`sort data.txt | uniq -u` prints the one unique line, which contains the next password. Log in to the next level with it.
 
 ## Why it works
-Sorting places identical lines next to each other, allowing `uniq` to identify repeated and unique records.
+Sorting imposes an order in which identical lines are necessarily adjacent. With duplicates grouped, `uniq -u`'s local adjacency test becomes a correct global answer: any line with no neighbor equal to itself occurs exactly once in the whole file.
+
+## Check yourself
+**Q1.** What would `uniq -u data.txt` (unsorted) most likely print, and why is it wrong?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Almost every line, because repeated lines are scattered and each isolated occurrence looks unique to `uniq`'s adjacency check. The count would claim hundreds of "unique" lines that are not.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** How would you instead get the *most common* line in a file?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-## Lessons learned
-Understand each pipeline stage; `uniq` alone does not find duplicates that are separated by other lines.
+`sort file | uniq -c | sort -nr | head -n 1`: count each distinct line, then rank numerically descending and take the top. (Same idiom as the log-analysis module.)
+</details>

-# Bandit Level 10 to Level 11: Base64 Decoding
+# Bandit Level 10 to Level 11: Base64 in the Wild
 
 
 ## Level objective
-Decode the Base64-encoded content described by the official level page.
+`data.txt` contains base64-encoded data. Decode it to read the password. Confirm the exact goal on the official level page.
 
 ## Concepts practiced
-Encoding versus encryption, Base64, and standard input/output.
+Recognizing and decoding base64, and understanding the difference between **encoding** and **encryption**.
+
+## Background
+Base64 is an *encoding*: a reversible byte-to-text scheme with a fixed, public alphabet and no key. It exists so binary data can travel through text-only channels (email bodies, JSON, configuration files). It is everywhere in real systems: certificates, API tokens, email attachments.
+
+Recognize it by shape: long runs of `A-Z a-z 0-9 + /`, often ending in `=` or `==` padding. Decode with the tool of the same name:
+
+```bash
+base64 -d data.txt
+```
+
+The critical security lesson: because the algorithm is public and keyless, **base64 is obfuscation, not protection**. Treating an encoded password as "encrypted" is one of the most common real-world mistakes; anyone who sees the string can decode it in a second, exactly as you are about to.
 
 ## Relevant commands
 ```bash
-base64 --decode data.txt
+file data.txt
+head -c 80 data.txt; echo
 base64 -d data.txt
 ```
 
 ## Hints (in increasing order)
-1. Base64 is an encoding scheme, not encryption.
-2. The `base64` utility supports a decode option.
-3. Decode the file and read the output; do not attempt to use the text as a key.
+1. The content is transformed, not hidden: a public, keyless scheme.
+2. The command shares the scheme's name.
+3. `-d` decodes.
 
 ## Solution explanation
-Run the Base64 decoder against the supplied file. The decoded output contains the next credential. Keep it private.
+`base64 -d data.txt` decodes the file and prints readable text including the next password. Use it to log in to the next level.
 
 ## Why it works
-Base64 maps binary data to a printable character alphabet. Decoding reverses that representation; it does not require a secret key.
+Base64 maps every three bytes to four characters from a 64-character alphabet, reversing deterministically. No secret is involved, so possession of the encoded text is possession of the content.
+
+## Check yourself
+**Q1.** A developer "secures" a token by base64-encoding it before storing it in a config file. What is wrong?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Encoding is not encryption: no key exists, so anyone with read access decodes it instantly. The token is still plaintext for every practical purpose; protect the file's permissions or use a real secret store.
+</details>
+
+**Q2.** What visual features make you suspect a string is base64?
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-## Lessons learned
-Encoding is not confidentiality. Never treat Base64 as a security boundary.
+The restricted alphabet (`A-Za-z0-9+/`), length in multiples of four, and `=`/`==` padding at the end.
+</details>

 function setupLessonInteractions(lesson) {
+  const completeButton = app.querySelector('[data-complete-lesson]');
+  if (completeButton && !progress.completed.includes(lesson.slug)) {
+    completeButton.addEventListener('click', async () => {
+      completeButton.disabled = true;
+      completeButton.textContent = 'Saving…';
+      let saved = true;
+      if (window.YaoguaiAccountUser && window.YaoguaiTraining) {
+        saved = await window.YaoguaiTraining.complete(`linux/${lesson.slug}`).catch(() => false);
+      }
+      if (saved) {
+        if (!progress.completed.includes(lesson.slug)) progress.completed.push(lesson.slug);
+        saveProgress();
+        render();
+      } else {
+        completeButton.disabled = false;
+        completeButton.textContent = 'Mark complete';
+      }
